# [CHAPTER 4Blockchain Explained](#contents.xhtml#ch04a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml#sc2_132a)

The previous chapter has been all about exploring AI: its definition, history, prospects, and human impact. Now it is time to focus on another emerging technology highlighted in this book in relation to AI, robotics, and automation. We already learned the basics of blockchain in [Chapter 1](#ch01.xhtml), Decentralization: The Foundations and Chapter 2, Technologies of Tomorrow, so it is time to dig deeper.

In this chapter, let us learn the history of crypto, and how it employed blockchain. Then, let us head on to a detailed explanation of the workings of the tech, its use cases outside of the crypto realm, and the building blocks when it comes to blockchain development.

First, let us discuss blockchain and how its history is tied with Bitcoin.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml#sc2_133a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Bitcoin and the history of blockchain
*   Bitcoin halving
*   Blockchain tech
*   Blockchain development
*   Becoming blockchain developer

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml#sc2_134a)

While we talk about the interconnection and possibilities of blockchain, AI, and robotics, it is important to learn about each individual technology closely. Since we have already read about AI, it is time for a spotlight on blockchains. This chapter covers the history and development of blockchain in relation to Bitcoin, along with a recounting of its fundamental concepts.

We hope by the end of this chapter, you would have a solid understanding of where blockchains came from and what makes a blockchain tick, and you would have an easier time comprehending how a combination of blockchain with other emerging technologies of the 2020’s make sense.

[Bitcoin and the history of blockchain](#contents.xhtml#sc2_135a)

The story of the blockchain of today starts with Bitcoin, the first crypto: an asset class that revolutionized digital finance. We can say that the creation of Bitcoin marked the genesis of blockchain technology today. Let us sum up the story so far with a brief overview:

*   Creation of Bitcoin: Bitcoin was introduced by the pseudonymous figure Satoshi Nakamoto in 2008, and it was the first decentralized cryptocurrency. Nakamoto created digital assets to rival fiat currencies—government-issued currency that is not backed by physical commodities like gold or silver—after the global financial crisis of 2008. This innovation aimed to give power over monetary affairs back to the general populace, without the oversight of a centralized organization, like banks.
    
    Bitcoin aimed to address issues with traditional financial systems by creating a transparent, peer-to-peer electronic financial system that operated without the need for intermediaries like banks, brokers, and more.
    
    Central to Bitcoin's innovation was its solution to the double-spending problem: the risk of spending the same digital currency unit more than once. All of Bitcoin’s core features from decentralization to transparency are attributed to blockchains.
    
*   Birth of the blockchain of today: To solve the double-spending problem and enable users to make secure, transparent transactions, Satoshi Nakamoto introduced the concept of a blockchain. Now, as you will remember from [Chapter 1](#ch01.xhtml), Decentralization: The Foundations, the notion of decentralized record-keeping in virtual blocks had existed before, but this was the first time an all-comprehensive data system was built based upon it.
    
    The blockchain underlying Bitcoin serves as a decentralized and distributed ledger that records all transactions in a chronological order with time stamps, and the relevant information regarding parties involved and amount transacted. Each block in the chain contains a set of transactions, and new blocks are always added at the end of the chain.
    
*   Blockchains are immutable: Any transaction recorded on the Bitcoin blockchain cannot be changed or tampered with in any way. The blocks of the Bitcoin blockchain are cryptographically linked together in a way that ensures the integrity of the entire chain.
    
    Immutability and transparency are some of the rules of blockchain we believe to be set in stone today, and it can be a surprising reminder that the Bitcoin blockchain itself gave birth to most of these inherent rules of Blockchain.
    
*   Bitcoin mining and proof of work: The Bitcoin blockchain relies on a consensus mechanism called proof of work (PoW): Bitcoin was the first blockchain to follow PoW, in fact. So, we can call proof of work the foundational consensus mechanism for blockchains, even though proof of stake seems to be a more sustainable option for today’s blockchains.

This is how the proof of work consensus mechanism works:

*   Bitcoin miners compete to solve complex mathematical puzzles with their high-powered specialized mining hardware.
*   Whoever wins gets to validate new transactions and add new blocks to the Bitcoin blockchain.
*   The use of PoW is essential in blockchain, as it: 
    *   Secures the network through validation.
    *   Creates new bitcoin as a reward for miners.
*   So new bitcoins are released into the system with this consensus mechanism. There will only ever be 21 million bitcoins in total, and Bitcoin mining is how they will be released until all bitcoins are out in circulation.
*   The PoW mechanism is designed to maintain scarcity and preserve value for BTC. This would contribute to the crypto’s value over time, and make it immune to traditional inflationary pressures, providing a potential hedge against currency devaluation.

To maintain the integrity and security of the Bitcoin network, complex mathematical puzzles and equations serve as a crucial component of the PoW mechanism. Solving these tasks successfully ensures only valid transactions are added to the blockchain. Let us discuss these mathematical puzzles to understand the competition in cryptocurrency mining:

*   Mathematical puzzle: Now, the puzzle Bitcoin miners solve involves a guessing game. The puzzles are there to require proof of computational work and energy expenditure from Bitcoin miners, which would in turn secure the Bitcoin network and prevent malicious actors from easily manipulating it.
    
    On the Bitcoin blockchain, miners compete to guess a value called a nonce (they try to guess the value exactly, or just reach as close as possible). The nonce combines data from the current block and the previous block and produces a hash value that meets certain criteria. This criterion is most often that the hash value produced should have a specific number of leading zeros.
    
    Here is how the competition for Bitcoin mining goes:
    
    *   The Bitcoin miners gather a batch of unconfirmed transactions carried out on the Bitcoin blockchain and create a block header, which is a data structure that includes several key components that record transactions, as shown in the following figure:
        
        ![](images/Figure_4.1-_Key_components_of_a_block.png)
        
        Figure 4.1: Key components of a block  
        Source: [https://media.geeksforgeeks.org/wp-content/uploads/20240829122823/blockchainstruct1.webp](https://media.geeksforgeeks.org/wp-content/uploads/20240829122823/blockchainstruct1.webp)
        
        The key components of the same are:
        
        *   Hash: It is a unique identifier for the block, generated through a cryptographic algorithm.
        *   Nonce: It is a number used by the miner to vary the hash output during hashing.
        *   Merkle root: It refers to the hash of transactions in the Merkle tree of the current block.
        *   Timestamp: It tells us the timestamp to place the block in the blockchain.
        *   Difficulty target: It is a measure of difficulty to find a valid hash that is equal to or less than the target.
        *   Previous block hash: It contains the hash (unique encrypted number) of the previous block in the blockchain.
            
            The preceding can be found at the following sources:
            
            [https://www.coinbase.com/learn/crypto-basics/what-is-mining](https://www.coinbase.com/learn/crypto-basics/what-is-mining)
            
            [https://www.investopedia.com/tech/how-does-bitcoin-mining-work/#:~:text=a%20cryptographic%20algorithm%20(-,called%20hashing,-).%20The%20result%20of](https://www.investopedia.com/tech/how-does-bitcoin-mining-work/#:~:text=a%20cryptographic%20algorithm%20\(-,called%20hashing,-\).%20The%20result%20of)
            
            [https://www.investopedia.com/terms/b/block-bitcoin-block.asp#:~:text=Previous%20block%20hash%3A](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/b/block-bitcoin-block.asp#:~:text=Previous%20block%20hash%3A)
            
    *   Now, the Bitcoin miners repeatedly change the nonce and hash the block header accordingly using a cryptographic hash function, which in Bitcoin's case is the SHA-256 algorithm.
    *   Now, the miners check if the hash coming out as a result meets the specific condition (as mentioned before, it is usually if the hash produced has a certain number of leading zeros). If the resulting hash still does not meet the required condition (i.e., it does not have the specified number of leading zeros), the miners adjust the nonce and try again. You can guess why proof of work is such an energy-intensive endeavor now, especially when you consider the environmental consequences.
    *   While miners keep searching for a hash to meet the specific conditions, the first miner to find a nonce that takes them close to a hash that satisfies the given condition immediately broadcasts the solution to the network. Now, the other Bitcoin miners test and verify the solution broadcasted by applying the hash function themselves.
    *   Once the solution is deemed valid, the Bitcoin block is mined, and the miner to have found and broadcasted the solution is rewarded with newly minted Bitcoin, as well as the transaction fees from the transactions included in the new block.

The competition in mining is essential because it adds substantial value to the Bitcoin blockchain and fortifies quite a few features of blockchains. Let us take a look at some of these features:

*   Immutable: The recorded transactions on the blockchain cannot be altered or deleted. This creates a permanent, tamper-proof ledger that enhances trust and security among users.
*   Decentralization: The absence of central authority reduces the risk of single-point failures and makes the network more resilient against attacks.
*   Security: Each transaction on the blockchain is cryptographically hashed and individually encrypted. This makes tampering with the original data nearly impossible because it would require changing all the subsequent hashes.
*   Consensus mechanism: Blockchain networks use complex algorithms that allow nodes to validate transactions and ensure that all changes are approved by a majority of validators.
*   Smart contracts: They are self-executing contracts that have the terms of the agreement directly embedded into codes. They automatically execute when all the pre-determined conditions are met and bypass the need for intermediaries or third-party intervention.
    
    Source: [https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/features-of-blockchain/?ref=lbp#:~:text=Course-,Features%20of%20Blockchain,-Last%20Updated%20%3A](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/features-of-blockchain/?ref=lbp#:~:text=Course-,Features%20of%20Blockchain,-Last%20Updated%20%3A)
    
    Here is why Bitcoin mining is necessary:
    
    *   Bitcoin mining ensures that new blocks are added to the blockchain at a controlled rate, and new bitcoins are released in circulation regularly.
    *   Mining prevents spamming of the network and requires participants to invest computational resources and time in exchange for the chance to add a new Bitcoin block and earn rewards.
    *   The difficulty of the mathematical puzzles in Bitcoin mining can be adjusted over time to maintain a consistent block generation rate, especially if the network's total computational power keeps fluctuating.
*   Decentralization and trustless-ness of blockchain: Bitcoin’s blockchain was the first decentralization ledger in broad effect for the good of the public. For instance, blockchain networks are transparent; all transactions are publicly visible on the distributed ledger. Such transparency helps build trust in the network as users can verify all the data. There is no single entity that controls the network, making it resistant to censorship and central authority.
    
    The Bitcoin blockchain also introduces a certain level of trustless-ness. Bitcoin transactions are executed peer-to-peer, and no one is required to place trust in their counterparty, intermediary, or a central authority for the transactions to be successfully concluded. This trustless-ness was a revolutionary feature to be introduced with blockchains, especially after a massive financial crisis of global scale like the one in 2008 that robbed people of their faith in centralized financial institutions.
    
    Source: [https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/features-of-blockchain/?ref=lbp#:~:text=2.-,Distributed,-All%20network%20participants](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/features-of-blockchain/?ref=lbp#:~:text=2.-,Distributed,-All%20network%20participants)
    
*   Bitcoin blockchain’s impact on the technology today: The Bitcoin blockchain was key in laying the foundation for a broader range of applications for blockchain beyond cryptocurrencies. Of course, it took developers some time to realize the potential of blockchain outside of the financial realm, but once they did, there was no stopping blockchain.
    
    So far, developers have worked to adapt the blockchain-based core decentralized system to other sectors like supply chain management, identity verification, smart contracts, and more.
    
*   Evolution of blockchain beyond Bitcoin: It is always a bittersweet feeling when talking about blockchain beyond Bitcoin, because prevalent thoughts of today from web3 developers are all about the shortcomings of Bitcoin. At some point we started blazing over the original, mind-blowing contribution of the Bitcoin blockchain in the thriving blockchain and web3 ecosystem we have today around the world.
    
    Sentimentalities aside, blockchain indeed did evolve past Bitcoin, even as it kept all its core concepts and functionalities intact. Blockchain platforms of today have adopted varied consensus mechanisms for heightened accessibility and sustainability, new models of governance for better efficiency and decentralization, and scalability solutions to address the limitations of the original PoW for an expanding network and adapt to different use cases.
    
*   Bitcoin’s contribution to the blockchain of today: However, in essence, the creation of Bitcoin indeed gave birth to the blockchain of today. Bitcoin revolutionized finance, showcasing blockchain’s efficiency in smoothening financial affairs for everyone, as well as hinting at the potential of blockchain beyond finance.
    
    While Bitcoin's primary application today remains as a store of value like gold and a decentralized currency to rival fiat, the innovation Bitcoin sparked has led to the exploration and development of a diverse range of blockchain-based solutions in the years since its initiation. Bitcoin spearheaded the web3 revolution of today, reshaping industries and creating broader and more inclusive opportunities across the world.
    
*   More about Bitcoin: Blockchain’s success in 2023 is tied in no small part to the success of Bitcoin so far. BTC is called the king of cryptocurrencies, and while the numerous crypto coins and tokens so far vary in terms of use cases, Bitcoin continues to be known as a worthy contender to fiat currencies around the world. Retail and institutional investors meanwhile consider Bitcoin a great source of value.

As the nations around the world contemplate the regulation of crypto, Bitcoin is already legal tender in the country of El Salvador. While 5G takes over the globe, BTC continues to bring new and inclusive financial solutions to unserved and underserved populations across countries.

[Bitcoin halving](#contents.xhtml#sc2_136a)

Bitcoin halving is a phenomenon built into crypto by Satoshi Nakamoto. It is an event that occurs approximately every four years or after every 210,000 blocks in the Bitcoin network. During a halving, the number of new bitcoins generated as rewards for miners after every successful block addition is cut in half.

When Bitcoin was launched in 2009, miners used to be rewarded with 50 bitcoins for every block they successfully mined. In 2012, the first Bitcoin halving occurred, reducing the block reward from 50 Bitcoins to 25 Bitcoins. The second halving took place four years later in 2016, further cutting the block reward in half from 25 Bitcoins to 12.5 Bitcoins. The next halving was then in May 2020, which cut down the block reward from 12.5 Bitcoins to 6.25 Bitcoins. After the 2024 Bitcoin halving, the Bitcoin block reward drops to 3.125 bitcoins per block.

In August 2023, there were already 19.45 million bitcoins in circulation, with 1.55 million bitcoins still left to be mined. Estimates suggest that it will be 2140 before all 21 million bitcoins are released into circulation.

The reason for the Bitcoin halving is controlled inflation, as well as implementing scarcity and increasing value into the currency.

Notably, Nakamoto themself mined about 1.1 million BTC between 2009 and 2010, and then disappeared, passing on the responsibility of BTC development to Gavin Andresen, formerly known as Gavin Bell.

Bitcoin is known for its extreme volatility, being a currency with its value riding on investor sentiments, as shown in the following figure:

![](images/Figure_4.2-_Bitcoin_volatility_over_time-_July_2013_to_Jan_2024.png)

Figure 4.2: Bitcoin volatility over time: July 2013 to Jan 2024  
Source: [https://www.vaneck.com/us/en/blogs/digital-assets/bitcoin-volatility/](https://www.vaneck.com/us/en/blogs/digital-assets/bitcoin-volatility/)

The volatility comes partly out of the Gartner Hype Cycle, which is a life cycle common for new technologies—after all, Bitcoin is still a teenager. The five stages of this cycle would be:

*   The technology trigger
*   The peak of inflated expectations
*   The trough of disillusionment
*   The slope of enlightenment
*   The plateau of productivity

Following this, Bitcoin has lost ground many times, but it has also returned to its previous high every time, and then surpassed it. Therefore, Bitcoin is not a bubble as many conventional investors still may fear, and a lot of the reason lies in Satoshi Nakamoto’s skillful use of blockchain. 

In 2023, with the rise of AI, blockchain is going to play a significant part in determining the authenticity of AI-driven content and ethical considerations for putting humans first. This would benefit both technologies and ensure public adoption. With rising market caps for Bitcoin and crypto, blockchain is definitely here to stay.

Gartner published a 2024 version of the hype cycle for blockchain and Web3. The following figure shows the details of the chart:

![](images/Figure_4.3-_Gartner_Hype_Cycle_stages_for_Web3_and_blockchain,_July_2024.png)

Figure 4.3: Gartner Hype Cycle stages for Web3 and blockchain, July 2024  
Source: [https://www.ledgerinsights.com/gartner-hype-cycle-stablecoins-to-go-mainstream-within-two-years/](https://www.ledgerinsights.com/gartner-hype-cycle-stablecoins-to-go-mainstream-within-two-years/)

One of the most significant aspects to note in [Figure 4.3](#ch04.xhtml#fig4-3) is that blockchain wallets, smart contracts, consensus mechanisms, decentralized applications, and cryptocurrencies are expected to reach a plateau of productivity in the next two years. It is also anticipated that they may gain high traction and adoption during this stage.

[Blockchain tech](#contents.xhtml#sc2_137a)

Let us go over what we know of the mechanics and functions of blockchain: at its core, blockchain is a distributed and decentralized digital ledger that is spread across a worldwide network of nodes and records and verifies transactions. To explain how the tech works, let us see how the core components come together to ensure smooth functioning:

*   Nodes facilitate efficient decentralization: As we know, a blockchain operates on a decentralized network of computers (nodes) rather than a single central authority. The nodes make the blockchain decentralized and ensure that no single entity has control over the entire network, enhancing security and preventing single points of failure.
*   Decentralized ledger ensures immutability: Once new data is added to a block and the block is added to the blockchain, the decentralized ledger of blockchains ensures all nodes have an updated copy of it, and that no bad actor can alter or delete any recorded data without network consensus. Thus, blockchain ensures the integrity of data, as well as faith from nodes through decentralization.
*   Cryptographic hash functions ensure security: Cryptographic hash functions are mathematical algorithms that convert any input data (such as transactions carried out on a blockchain) into strings of characters of a certain length (the hashes). Every hash is unique to the input data on a blockchain and takes several factors into consideration, which are the transactions to be recorded, a timestamp, the hash of the previous block, and a nonce. The hashes are used for identification and verification purposes on a blockchain, and they ensure security and transparency.
    
    Further, the hashing ensures compression of quite a large volume of data and saves storage space for full nodes on a blockchain that must keep updated copies of the ledger. Notably, the hashing ensures immutability too: a bad actor cannot change a block’s hash without causing alarm to all nodes. This is because the chaining of blocks through hash references, along with cryptographic hashing properties, makes sure that any modification to a block's content or would lead to detectable changes throughout the blockchain, since a block’s hash carries a reference to its previous block.
    
    Thus, hashing contributes to the tamper-resistant nature of the blockchain and maintains the immutability and security of blockchain data.
    
*   Blocks group together transactions: The blocks of transactions a blockchain is known for are meant to group together data related to individual transactions. Each block on a blockchain contains a list of transactions, a timestamp, a reference to the previous block's hash (to create an immutable chain of blocks), and a nonce (a number used to ensure a specific condition when hashing a block). The blocks make it easier to find information on a blockchain, and their contents are visible to all network participants, ensuring transparency.
*   Consensus mechanisms ensure decentralized governance: Consensus mechanisms, as we know, determine how all nodes on a blockchain reach agreement on the validity of transactions and the order in which they are added to the blockchain. The PoW originated with Bitcoin and the newer Proof of Stake (PoS) Ethereum 2.0 operates on common consensus mechanisms to ensure all nodes’ voices are heard in network decisions.
    
    Transactions on a blockchain are validated by nodes on the network using these consensus mechanisms. Transactions need to adhere to predefined rules laid down by the consensus mechanism before the nodes accept them and record them into a block.
    
    All consensus mechanisms have a certain way to ensure the nodes are truly dedicated to the progress of the blockchain network. For example, PoW demands miners to put in enough computational effort through competing to solve the mathematical puzzle related to guessing the correct nonce, and PoS demands that nodes stake a certain minimum amount of the native crypto for a certain period to compete and qualify as a block validator for every block added to the chain.
    
*   Smart contracts bring automation and reduce human intervention: Smart contracts are one of the features, the original Bitcoin blockchain did not have; they were brought forward by Ethereum in 2015. As we know, smart contacts are self-executing contracts with terms agreed upon by counterparties in a transaction and directly written into code. After activation, they automatically execute these actions upon the predefined conditions being met by the counterparties. These contracts are great when it comes to ensuring automated, fast, trustless transactions, and they are handy for both traders and developers in blockchain.
*   Peer-to-peer networks ensure smooth communication: Nodes on a blockchain communicate with each other in a peer-to-peer fashion. This is how they validate transactions, add new blocks to the chain and update their copies of the ledger, and maintain the overall integrity of the blockchain. The following [Figure 4.4](#ch04.xhtml#fig4-4) shows a graphical representation of peer-to-peer networks:
    
    ![](images/Figure_4.4-_Bitcoin_blockchain_originates_most_features_of_the_modern_blockchain.png)
    
    Figure 4.4: Bitcoin blockchain originates most features of the modern blockchain  
    Source: [https://s3.cointelegraph.com/storage/uploads/view/d6015d82025b7e1151290089b0fd45e2.png](https://s3.cointelegraph.com/storage/uploads/view/d6015d82025b7e1151290089b0fd45e2.png)
    
*   Public and private keys ensure security for network participants: A blockchain makes use of asymmetric cryptography. Therefore, each user on the chain is provided with a pair of keys attached to their virtual wallet, one being a public key you can share to receive transactions (like a UPI ID) and the other being a private key you can use to sign transactions you initiate and prove ownership over the crypto in your account (like your UPI PIN). This mechanism ensures security for all nodes on the blockchain network, making a decentralized system without centralized supervisors safe.
*   Blockchain forking solves immutability-related challenges: Now these are not exactly one of the essential components of a blockchain, but they are an important part of the technology, nonetheless. Blockchain forks come when the nodes disagree regarding any governance decisions, decisions involving the functioning and use case of the blockchain, and about the validity of transactions. Since blockchains are immutable, forks are there to initiate any changes or upgrades.

Blockchain forks are mainly of two types: soft forks and hard forks. These forks come about for reasons like adding new features, enhancing security, or addressing other issues.

[Soft fork in a blockchain](#contents.xhtml#sc3_138a)

A soft blockchain fork is a backward-compatible upgrade to a blockchain protocol. Through a soft fork, developers might look to introduce new rules that are more restrictive than the existing rules of the blockchain.

In a soft fork, the original participants of the blockchain can still participate in the new network without moving from the basic platform. The new rules implemented through a soft fork are compatible with the older rules a blockchain was based upon, which ensures that old nodes can validate the transactions and blocks created by new nodes.

Here are the key features of a soft fork briefly:

*   New rules are more restrictive in some way than the existing rules.
*   Older blockchain nodes can still validate new blocks after the soft fork, even if they do not understand or support the new rules.
*   Over 50% of blockchain miners need to agree to the new rules for the soft fork to be successful. However, it would go forward with support from the majority even if not all nodes agree.
*   There is no requirement for all nodes to upgrade immediately after a blockchain soft fork.

[Hard fork](#contents.xhtml#sc3_139a)

A blockchain hard fork is a more substantial and potentially disruptive upgrade to a blockchain protocol compared to a soft fork. The hard fork involves introducing brand new rules that are not backward compatible with the existing rules of the blockchain.

What this means is that the blockchain nodes that have not upgraded to the new protocol will no longer be able to validate transactions and blocks created by nodes on the new protocol. A hard fork usually leads to a split in the blockchain and creates a separate network on the basic code of the original one. A blockchain hard fork thus results in two separate chains, each following different sets of rules.

Here are the key features of a hard fork briefly:

*   The new rules introduced through the hard fork are not compatible with the existing rules, leading to a divergence in the blockchain platform.
*   The old nodes that still follow the old rules cannot validate transactions and blocks added by nodes on the new ledger.
*   All nodes must upgrade to the new protocol to avoid disruptions and participate in the new platform.
*   A hard fork might also require a new native cryptocurrency for the new platform if the split leads to changes in consensus mechanisms and such major changes.

Both blockchain soft forks and hard forks are mechanisms used to upgrade and evolve existing blockchain networks. Since many are talking about green Bitcoin, for example, if the community decides to introduce a more sustainable consensus mechanism for Bitcoin, it will need a hard fork for it to be implemented.

The choice between a hard fork and a soft fork depends on the disruptive nature of the changes being introduced in the blockchain, and the consensus among network participants. Even if they simply require a majority vote to be implemented, it is still important to plan and communicate forks well to minimize disruptions for all participants and maintain the decentralization and overall convenience of the blockchain ecosystem.

Let us take a quick look at some examples of notable blockchain forks.

[Examples of blockchain forks](#contents.xhtml#sc3_140a)

Here are some popular and disruptive examples of blockchain forks we have witnessed so far:

*   The Bitcoin Cash (BCH) fork: 
    *   Type: Hard fork
    *   Date: August 1, 2017
    *   Reason for forking: The Bitcoin network had philosophical and technical disagreements on block size scaling. While those wanting the change preferred larger block sizes to accommodate more transactions quickly, the other faction wanted smaller blocks like usual, with some transactions sent to off-chain solutions like the Bitcoin Lightning Network for increased scalability. The two factions did not come to a mutual agreement, and so those wanting larger blocks created a hard fork.
    *   Outcome: Bitcoin Cash was created as a separate blockchain with bigger block sizes (8MB initially, and later increased). Bitcoin Cash aims to provide faster, and cheaper transactions compared to the original Bitcoin network. The new native token for Bitcoin Cash is called BCH.
*   Segregated Witness (SegWit) activation on Bitcoin: 
    *   Type: Soft fork
    *   Date: August 24, 2017
    *   Reason: Initiated just a few days after the divergence of Bitcoin and Bitcoin Cash, the soft fork called SegWit, aimed to provide a solution to Bitcoin's scalability issues by storing transaction data separately from the counterparty signature data, allowing for more space in a block for transactions. The idea for this development was originally proposed in 2015. Bitcoin Cash was forked to avoid the protocol updates Segregated Witness brought about.
    *   Outcome: Upon activation as a soft fork, SegWit made way for further developments like the integration of the Bitcoin Lightning Network, which aims to enable faster and cheaper off-chain transactions.
*   Ethereum Classic (ETC) fork: 
    *   Type: Hard fork
    *   Date: July 20, 2016
    *   Reason: In response to the infamous DAO hack, in which about $60 million’s worth of ETH was stolen, the Ethereum community had to perform a hard fork to do damage control.
    *   Outcome: A big majority of the then Ethereum community spoke in support of the fork and moved to the new chain (the Ethereum we know now). However, a minority of the community members still disagreed and continued on the original chain, which became known as Ethereum Classic. The blockchain’s native token is called ETC.
*   Byzantium upgrade on Ethereum: 
    *   Type: Soft fork
    *   Date: October 16, 2017
    *   Reason: The Byzantium upgrade was done as a part of the broader Metropolis upgrade on Ethereum, with the goal to improve the network's scalability, privacy, and security.
    *   Outcome: This one, being a soft fork, did not require a separate platform to be made. The Byzantium upgrade ended up initiating several improvements, including shifts in Ethereum gas pricing, a decrease in block rewards, and enhanced security measures, as it set out to do.
*   Monero hard forks: 
    *   Type: Hard fork
    *   Date: Multiple
    *   Reason: The Monero community has till date carried out several hard forks to put protocol upgrades into use and improve many privacy-related attributes.
    *   Outcome: These forks introduced changes like enhanced privacy with features like Ring Confidential Transactions (Ring CT), decreased transaction sizes, and better security measures.
*   Ethereum 2.0 and the Shanghai-Capella upgrade: 
    *   Type: Hard fork
    *   Date: April 12, 2023
    *   Reason: Now, all of us have certainly heard of this one, even if we do not know the full details. The Merge took place in 2022 that finally converted the consensus mechanism for the Ethereum blockchain from PoW to PoS. While this made the network save up on over 99% of the energy, it was using as a PoW blockchain, the main net release of the Ethereum Shanghai Capella upgrade took one step further in improving all aspects of Ethereum. The Shanghai-Capella upgrade or Shapella hard fork mainly focuses on staked ETH withdrawals from the deposit contract.
    *   Outcome: Several Ethereum Improvement Proposals (EIPs) are now in effect with the Shapella upgrade. EIP-4895 is now allowing withdrawal of staked funds, EIP-3855 is bringing down gas fees, and EIP-3860 aims to cap developer gas fees in certain cases.

All these examples of blockchain forks show the diverse reasons that lead blockchain communities to initiate soft or hard blockchain forks. From severe disagreements over protocol’s workings to minimizing security vulnerabilities to solving scalability issues, blockchain forks are required as solutions for many reasons. While not all are as successful as the nodes hope for, they are certainly an excellent countermeasure for the restrictions caused by the immutability aspect of blockchains!

Now that we know which components come together to give the blockchain technology its features, it is time to look at blockchain beyond crypto.

[Non-crypto use cases of blockchain](#contents.xhtml#sc3_141a)

Blockchain having made it far past just Bitcoin and crypto in 2023, and now has numerous non-crypto currencies use cases across various industries. The excellence of most of these use cases can be attributed to the transparency, security, and decentralization of blockchains.

Without further ado, let us look at some examples.

[Non-crypto industries using blockchain](#contents.xhtml#sc4_142a)

First, a quick look at the many sectors blockchain has already spread around the world, some of which we already discussed in the first chapter. Even if all of the use cases are not realized yet, developers have already visualized them, and they are in the process of being tested out.

Here are blockchain’s non-crypto use cases:

*   Supply chain management: Blockchain can bring end-to-end visibility and traceability in supply chains. The mechanisms of blockchain enable precise tracking of products' origins, processing, and distribution, enhancing transparency and reducing fraud across industries like agriculture, pharmaceuticals, and other goods and services.
*   Agriculture and food safety: The blockchain tech can ensure food safety by tracking the journey of all agricultural products from farm to table, helping identify the cause of and thereafter manage contamination or other issues.
*   Healthcare data sharing: Blockchain can securely store and share patients’ medical records, ensuring data integrity and privacy, as well as accessibility. Patients, doctors, healthcare providers, and even researchers can access and share accurate information related to patients while sharing equal control over data access.
*   Real estate records and transactions: Blockchain can easily streamline property transactions by securely recording real estate titles, ownership history, and legal documents, and putting them online for all stakeholders to see. This cuts down on paperwork, increases efficiency, and prevents fraud.
*   Identity verification: The blockchain tech can create self-sovereign digital identities for users, allowing people to exert control over their personal data and share it securely with authorized parties only when they want to, such as financial institutions or government agencies.
*   Logistics management: Blockchain can work to optimize logistics by bringing real-time tracking of shipments, reducing delays, and improving the overall efficiency of the transportation and logistics industry with decentralization, peer-to-peer communication, and transparency.
*   Voting systems: Blockchain can facilitate transparent and tamper-proof digital elections, doing away with electoral fraud prevalent in every part of the world, and enhancing voter confidence and safety. Each vote through blockchains can be recorded securely, ensuring the integrity of election results, and making them tamper-proof.
*   Protection for intellectual property: The decentralized ledger of blockchains can be used to timestamp and verify the ownership and creation date of any intellectual property, the likes of patents, copyrights, and trademarks.
*   Digital content and copyright issues: With blockchains, artists and content creators have a new way to prove ownership and track the usage and distribution of the digital content they put out, ensuring fair compensation and copyright enforcement for them.
*   Energy trading and management: Blockchain can enable peer-to-peer energy trading, allowing individuals and businesses to buy and sell redundant energy directly from each other, and manage their energy consumption much more efficiently.
*   Donations: In recent years, we have heard of many frauds where scammers raised a lot of money in the name of charitable donations and ran off with the accumulated funds. Blockchain can enhance transparency in these charitable organizations and provide a tamper-proof record of donations and their allocation, so donors can check and verify the legitimacy of the cause.
*   Insurance claims: In the financial space, blockchain can work to automate and accelerate insurance claims processing by allowing the secure sharing of data among insurers, policyholders, and service providers.
*   Legal contracts: With smart contracts, legal contracts can better ensure integrity and the meeting of conditions. Further, blockchain can streamline the creation, execution, and then enforcement of legal contracts by facilitating a tamper-proof record of agreements, decreasing disputes and reducing the need for human intermediaries.

All these use cases highlight how the decentralized and secure nature of blockchains serves to revolutionize various industries. So, you might also be able to brainstorm some ideas about how blockchains can be used in other industries that we might have missed here.

There are several notable non-crypto applications of blockchain as of 2024.

[Non-crypto applications of blockchain](#contents.xhtml#sc4_143a)

Let us start with blockchain in food safety.

*   Blockchain in food safety: Many giant brands around the world use blockchain to ensure the food they provide to their customers is safe, some of them being: 
    *   Walmart: Walmart has made use of blockchain in the food chain to track packages of sliced mango. While it used to take them six days to manually track the whole journey of said mango, with blockchain, they brought the time down to mere seconds.
    *   Nestlé: Nestlé used blockchain to trace milk from the farms to the production houses to the factories. This has helped them ensure transparency and integrity of their products, as well as earn praise for honest business practices.
    *   JBS: JBS has made use of blockchain in food for efficient cross-checking of livestock transportation information from suppliers, enhancing security, transparency, and traceability.
    *   Tyson Foods: Tyson Foods has tried out blockchain to track their supply chain from farms and production houses to factories. Again, this ensures heightened and easy traceability. 
*   Blockchain in government initiatives: Quite a few government entities have made use of blockchain so far, and here are some of the solutions in use: 
    *   Kaleido: Kaleido is a brand bringing blockchain-based solutions for the integration of distributed ledgers across industries. Kaleido has provided services to the United Nations, Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, and also the World Wide Fund for Nature.
    *   Follow My Vote: This one facilitates safe online voting through an open-source ballot box built on blockchain. Follow My Vote ensures easy accessibility for voters, as well as voter safety and trust in the electoral process
    *   Voatz: Voatz is another mobile voting platform that is based upon a blockchain. They bring an encrypted biometric security system that allows voters to use their smartphones for voting from any corner of the world with a semi-decent internet connection. Voatz again ensures voter privacy and trust in the voting system.
*   Blockchain smart contracts in non-crypto use: Yes, smart contracts have great potential outside of the crypto space. Here are the real-world examples: 
    *   Google: Google brings web3 solutions like its Blockchain Node Engine, which provides smart contracts related services. The Blockchain Node Engine allows developers to manage their blockchain nodes, complete transactions smoothly, and deploy smart contracts for their usage with support from the Google Cloud network.
    *   DFINITY: DFINITY’s Internet Computer is an open-source blockchain system that lets users build decentralized applications on-chain, with the help of highly scalable smart contracts. 
*   Blockchain in healthcare: Blockchain technology has various real-world applications across various sectors. Let us take a look: 
    *   BurstIQ: This one helps doctors manage and collect insights relevant to sensitive health information for patients. Each smart contract provided by the platform imparts full data ownership to users, allowing them the choice to decide how and with whom their information is shared for personalized healthcare.
    *   Medicalchain: Medicalchain allows an easier and secure flow of data in healthcare. With this, doctors do not require to wait for insurance information related to a patient to be fetched manually, the blockchain-based application can instead quickly check if a patient has insurance.
    *   Patientory: This is a comprehensive medical record keeping system for both patients and doctors, based upon blockchain. It allows healthcare providers to instantaneously access a patient’s medical history and provide them with the care needed.
    *   WholeCare: WholeCare is another blockchain-based function streamlining patient healthcare. It brings customized care plans, medication protocols, easy ways of booking appointments, and other resources, in the decentralized and secure way of blockchains.
*   Blockchain in real estate management: Blockchain is also used for real estate management in the real-world, here is how: 
    *   Propy INC: Propy is a real estate marketplace spread worldwide that allows you to buy and sell properties the web3 way. Smart contracts are used to make these transactions immutable and safe, and Propy even allows for real estate to be made into non-fungible tokens (NFTs) and traded.
*   Blockchain in logistics: Let us discuss the real-world applications: 
    *   Oracle: Oracle has an application called the Intelligent Track and Trace for supply chain management, based upon blockchains. The software allows multi-tier transparency for supply chains, so users can track the quality and shipping status of food easily.
    *   DHL: DHL, the well-known logistics company with a large presence in the US, has embraced blockchain-based logistics for a while now. The company uses blockchain to maintain its digital ledger of shipments and ensure the integrity of transactions carried out.

Now, these are the many examples of blockchain in the real world beyond cryptocurrencies. You can clearly note how in just over a decade, blockchain technology has overtaken most of the industries related to our daily activities, and ensured its core principles are in place to improve the quality of our lives.

All these real-world use cases and applications of blockchain clearly prove that contrary to general opinion, blockchain has gone mainstream already. When some of us say that it has not, we must remember that other better accepted technologies like AI and automation also have a long way to go to meet our expectations of the mainstream: which simply means the technology being used directly by us, the common people, and for us to understand said technology.

In fact, this widespread usage can be ensured with better education and awareness of the features and benefits of blockchain, which will make it seem less alien. Of course, this goes for the other emerging technologies as well.

Now, the final leg of our chapter focuses on how to approach blockchain development.

[Blockchain development](#contents.xhtml#sc2_144a)

A discussion on blockchains would be incomplete without telling you how blockchain development can be approached, especially for those of you considering going on to web3-relevant tech development. That is why the final section of this chapter is dedicated to the basics of developing a blockchain.

[Key stages of blockchain development](#contents.xhtml#sc3_145a)

Developing a blockchain application comprises quite a few technical and conceptual steps. Here are the significant steps you must consider, in a watered-down version of course:

1.  Define the use case and requirements: The first step to developing a blockchain application is determining the issue you are looking to solve with it, and of course, the sector or industry it will be developed for. Remember to note down the specific requirements and features your blockchain needs to have to address the use case.
    
2.  Choose the blockchain type: If you would remember, in the first chapter, we had discussed the three main types of blockchain: public, private, and consortium or federated blockchains. Based on your particular use case, you would need to pick whether your blockchain platform will be open to the public or restricted to specific participants.
    
3.  Pick a consensus mechanism: Next, you will need to pick a consensus mechanism that goes perfectly with your use case.
    
    | Tip: In 2024 and beyond, picking a sustainable consensus protocol like proof of stake will take you a long way, and it is convenient for most use cases. |
    | --- |
    
4.  Visualize the blockchain architecture: Now you need to picture and define the various components of your blockchain, namely nodes and native crypto, and determine how transactions will be carried out and which roles are to be assigned to the nodes.
    
5.  Pick a development platform: Now you need to choose a suitable blockchain platform or framework based on your chosen blockchain type. For example, if you are going with a public blockchain, Ethereum and Binance Smart Chain are common choices. For private blockchains, however, you can look at platforms like Hyperledger Fabric or Corda.
    
6.  Decide whether you need smart contracts: First you need to determine if your use case needs automation enabled by smart contracts. If yes, you can develop them using a programming language compatible with your chosen blockchain platform for development, so, for example, you can use Solidity for an Ethereum-based public blockchain protocol.
    
7.  Build the basic network infrastructure: You need to set up the blockchain network by deploying nodes that will participate in the consensus mechanism you have chosen. You also need to configure nodes with the required software and appropriate security measures.
    
8.  Coding and development for the blockchain: Next up is implementing the core components of your blockchain, including consensus mechanisms, data structures, transaction validation procedures, and any networking protocols.
    
9.  Testing and quality assurance for various aspects: It is time to test the blockchain application you have put together so far: now you run it in a testing environment to identify and fix bugs, vulnerabilities, and any performance issues. For that, you would have to conduct unit tests, integration tests, and end-to-end tests to make sure your blockchain application is functioning as you wanted it to.
    
10.  Deployment and launch of the blockchain: Tests are all done and your blockchain passed with flying colors. Or maybe it did not work as intended at first, but then you worked on the shortcomings and now it is better than ever. Now you deploy your blockchain network on the environment of your choice, whether it is a testnet or a mainnet.
     
     A testnet is a blockchain environment specifically designed for testing and experimentation purposes. It helps you identify and fix any issues with your blockchain application before you send it over to the mainnet.
     
     On the other hand, the mainnet is the main network, or the live and operational blockchain network where real transactions and activities are undertaken by users. So, a testnet is a sandbox-like environment you can use for testing and experimentation, while the mainnet should be for the final version of the application you have built, so users can participate on the platform at the lowest possible risk.
     
     While you launch your blockchain, do ensure the correct setup of nodes, your consensus mechanism, and very importantly, the security measures.
     
11.  Monitoring and maintenance of the blockchain: Do remember to regularly monitor the health and performance of your blockchain network. Promptly address any issues that arise after consulting with all participants and apply the agreed upon updates as needed. You can consider forking to implement the changes.
     
12.  Community and ecosystem building: If your blockchain is a public one, fostering a community around your blockchain is very important. Remember to engage with enthusiasts on social media platforms and encourage developers in particular to build decentralized applications or dApps of their choice on top of your blockchain platform.
     
13.  Documentation and support for your blockchain: While you go for community building, remember to put out well-crafted, comprehensive documentation regarding your project online for potential users, developers, and contributors. Further, provide support channels where users can share their queries and any issues they face, and be prompt in answering these communications.
     

Now, that was a simplified explanation of the long and complex process involved in developing a blockchain. Of course, developing a blockchain requires a solid understanding of the technology, as well as solid programming skills and domain-specific experience.

Let us discuss in brief how you can become a blockchain developer.

[Becoming a blockchain developer](#contents.xhtml#sc2_146a)

Becoming a blockchain developer will take you to a solid combination of relevant education, technical skills, hands-on experience, and a deep understanding of blockchain technology. Here is a step-by-step guide that you can follow to start your journey as a blockchain developer:

*   Learn the basics of Blockchain technology: You can start the journey by gaining a solid understanding of how blockchain technology works, including inherent concepts like decentralization, consensus mechanisms, cryptography, hashing, smart contracts, and more. You can do this by enrolling in online courses and tutorials that specialize in blockchain education and development.
*   Familiarize yourself with cryptocurrencies: The understanding of blockchain is intertwined with cryptocurrencies, so remember to learn thoroughly about different cryptocurrencies, especially the workings of the most popular ones like Bitcoin and Ethereum. Take care to comprehend their use cases and the underlying technologies.
*   Learn related programming languages: You need to master some of the programming languages commonly used in blockchain development as well, such as Solidity for Ethereum smart contracts, JavaScript, Python, Rust, and others. As most experts would tell you, Solidity is particularly important if you want to create smart contracts. It is one of the easier programming languages.
*   Understand data structures and algorithms: Now you need to develop a strong understanding of data structures and algorithms related to blockchains, since they are essential to efficiently develop blockchains.
*   Explore different blockchain platforms and frameworks: You can attempt to get hands-on experience in blockchain development with popular platforms that welcome developers like Ethereum, Binance Smart Chain, and more. For the best experience in developing, experiment with the blockchain development frameworks and tools provided they provide you with.
*   Understand blockchain smart contracts: Smart contracts are very important to learn about if you want to develop a blockchain-based application today, so read up on the concept of smart contracts and internalize how they work. You should learn how to write, deploy, and interact with smart contracts on various blockchain platforms.
*   Learn to develop decentralized apps: After smart contracts, you can explore decentralized application or dApp development to build interactive platforms upon blockchain networks. Learning dApp development would teach you the nitty-gritties of creating user interfaces, deploying smart contracts, and implementing mechanisms for transaction handling.
*   Start building your own projects: Start building your own blockchain projects to apply your knowledge so far. The hands-on experience should help you hone your practical blockchain skills, while also showcasing your work to any potential employers.
*   Start experimenting with testnets: Now, you can utilize test networks provided by various blockchain platforms to practice development without any real stakes. This way you can test your application in a safe environment.
*   Contribute to open-source projects: While you test your skills as a blockchain developer, you can also participate in open-source blockchain projects to collaborate with other developers and learn from them.
*   Networking and collaboration: On a learning note, keep an eye on your local blockchain developments through social media and attend blockchain meetups, conferences, and forums to network with professionals in the field. This collaboration and knowledge sharing will certainly enhance your growth as a blockchain developer.
*   Stay updated with the world of blockchain: Blockchain technology is rapidly evolving, so remember to stay updated with the latest trends, developments, and advancements in blockchain, cryptocurrency, and the overall web3 space.

Now, as easy as these steps were to read, it is important to remember that becoming a proficient blockchain developer takes considerable time and dedication. So, while you start at the basic education, steer yourself to keep at it until you have the level of skills needed to become a true, professional blockchain developer.

Blockchain is an emerging technology with a lot of visible potential, even as it fundamentally came out of the crypto space. While cryptocurrencies explore new frontiers of digital transactions in finance, blockchain will run ahead and collaborate with other technologies to shape our future.

In the current times, becoming a blockchain professional would lead you to become a core part of the upcoming technological revolution, not to mention the hefty financial compensation for your role alongside it.

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml#sc2_147a)

Blockchain has worked wonders for cryptocurrencies, and it has also spilled over to our everyday industries to bring heightened transparency and better user control, among other things. We hope this chapter has been instrumental in helping you understand blockchain and its implications a little better, and you are going to have an easier time grasping the sheer potential of the combination of AI, blockchain, robotics, and other up and coming technologies that we discuss in this book.

In this chapter, we have learned all the basics of blockchain- from its origin underlying the crypto Bitcoin to the core components of blockchain, along with some real-world use cases of blockchain beyond crypto. Further, we also learned how the development of blockchain is done. 

Now that we have explored AI and blockchain, in the next chapter, we will do the same with robotics. Let us find out the history, future, and current use cases of robotics.

[Questions](#contents.xhtml#sc2_148a)

1.  How is Bitcoin related to the genesis of blockchain?
    
    Answer: Bitcoin was the first implementation of the blockchain we know today. Therefore, Bitcoin as a crypto is what introduced blockchain to the world.
    
2.  Which are the core features of the Bitcoin blockchain?
    
    Answer: The core features of the Bitcoin blockchain include:
    
    *   Nodes for decentralization
    *   Mining mechanism and proof of work consensus algorithm
    *   Cryptographically secure
    *   Immutable
    *   Transparent and decentralized
    *   Blocks group together transactions
3.  What is a consensus mechanism? Why does a blockchain need one?
    
    Answer: A consensus mechanism is used on a blockchain to make sure the majority of nodes (participants) agree on a certain decision. Without a consensus mechanism like proof of work, a blockchain would not be truly decentralized.
    
4.  Which are the core components of a blockchain?
    
    Answer: The core components of a blockchain are:
    
    *   Blocks
    *   Nodes
    *   Cryptographic hashes
    *   Crypto wallets, and private and public keys
    *   Consensus mechanism
    *   Smart contracts
    *   Peer-to-peer networks
    *   Decentralized ledger
5.  What is blockchain forking?
    
    Answer: Blockchain forks are upgrades that take place when a majority of nodes can not agree on a certain network decision. These forks can be a soft fork (an upgrade to the original blockchain) or a hard fork (a new blockchain is created to implement the new decision while the old one operates as it always had).
    
6.  Which are the main differences between a blockchain hard fork and a soft fork?
    
    Answer: While a blockchain soft fork does not require nodes to onboard a new blockchain after the upgrade, a hard fork needs nodes to move to the new blockchain depending on whether they oppose or support the new decision.
    
7.  Which are some non-crypto use cases of blockchain?
    
    Answer: Blockchain can be used across a variety of industries, namely:
    
    *   It can be used for greater transparency in supply chain management.
    *   Blockchains can be used for record-keeping and management in healthcare, so patients can access their personal records at any time and healthcare institutes can also access them at the earliest possible instance without asking the patient for them in a critical condition.
8.  Give some examples of non-crypto real-world applications of blockchain.
    
    Answer: Here are a few non-crypto usage of blockchains so far in the real world:
    
    *   Walmart has used blockchain in the food chain to keep track of packages of sliced mango.
    *   Google’s Blockchain Node Engine brings smart contracts related services.
    *   BurstIQ helps doctors manage and gain information regarding patients’ issues with the help of blockchain.
9.  What are the key stages in the development of a blockchain?
    
    Answer: The key stages in the development of blockchains include:
    
    *   Pick use case
    *   Choose the type of blockchain required
    *   Choose a consensus mechanism
    *   Create the blockchain architecture
    *   Choose an existing blockchain platform/infrastructure to carry out your use case
    *   Implement smart contracts if need be
    *   Configure nodes
    *   Set up consensus mechanisms, data structures, transaction validation procedures, and networking protocols
    *   Put the features to test
    *   Launch your blockchain
    *   Keep monitoring the project
    *   Build a community
    *   Create documentation for your blockchain
    *   Stay open for user inputs
10.  Do you believe formal education is necessary to become a blockchain developer?
     
     Answer: As with any new sector, the earliest professionals usually do not possess formal education and degrees relevant to their field. However, at this stage, a course on your particular area in blockchain developments can certainly help you out.

# [CHAPTER 5Robotics Explained](#contents.xhtml#ch05a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml#sc2_149a)

Now that we have explored blockchain and AI, let us understand robotics.

In this chapter, we are going to explore where mechanical engineering, electronic engineering, and computer science converge, transforming our lives in more ways than we often realize—robotics. Robots are no longer just the stuff of science fiction. They are here, and they are becoming increasingly integral to our everyday lives. From manufacturing our goods and taking care of our health to helping us around the house and even exploring the farthest reaches of space, robots are stepping in to do the tasks that are dangerous, dull, or simply beyond human capabilities. They are enhancing efficiency, precision, and safety across various sectors, making our lives easier and safer.

The evolution from the first visions of automated machines to the complex robots we see today is driven by continuous advancements in technology, engineering, and computer science. That's a fascinating journey marked by incredible innovations, ambitious dreams, and the tireless efforts of scientists and engineers around the globe. This chapter will explore robotics’ history, significance, design and functionality in detail. We will also understand various use cases and look at some of the popular robots worldwide. Lastly, we will also discuss about a few potential career opportunities for those who want to enter this transformative field.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml#sc2_150a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Introducing robotics
*   Exploring the significance of robotics
*   Robotics and its history
*   Iterations in robotics design and functionality
*   Industrial use cases of robotics
*   Domestic use cases of robotics
*   Famous robots around the world
*   Career in robotics

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml#sc2_151a)

In this chapter, we are going to explore the fascinating evolution of robotics, from its earliest origins to the advanced, learning machines we see today. We will also understand why we need robots, how they have transformed from simple automated machines to complex entities, and the key milestones that have shaped this field.

However, our journey does not stop at history. We are also going to explore how robotics technology has revolutionized various industries and our daily lives. From manufacturing and logistics to healthcare and agriculture, robots are making a significant impact. They are not just confined to factories and fields—they are also helping us in our homes, assisting with tasks, and even providing entertainment in the form of robotic toys.

By the end of this chapter, we hope you will have a comprehensive understanding of the field of robotics, and you will appreciate the potential of robotics technology and its impact on various sectors and everyday life.

[Introducing robotics](#contents.xhtml#sc2_152a)

Today, robotics is one of the most intriguing and rapidly evolving fields in technology. It converges technologies like computer engineering, artificial intelligence, and automation to create highly advanced automated machines that can perform sophisticated tasks in a controlled manner. Robots have infiltrated many areas of our lives and are here to evolve, whether we realize it or not.

A robot typically consists of several key components that allow it to interact and respond to its environment:

*   Sensors: Robots use advanced sensors like cameras, Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR), infrared and ultrasonic sensors, pressure detectors, etc., to sense and interact with their surroundings.
*   Actuators: These function as robotic muscles and convert electrical inputs into actual movements. Mechanical parts such as motors, servos, and pneumatic actuators help in robotic arm movements.
*   Control systems: They are the brain of the robots that process data and provide relevant output. The software enables real-time decision making, allowing robots to adapt and perform tasks with precision.
*   Power supply: Robots require extensive power for mobility and autonomy. Fuel cells often provide longer-lasting energy. Though power cables provide reliable electricity, they limit movement.
*   Communication systems: These allow robots to interact with humans or other robots effectively via wireless technologies like Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, or custom protocols. Such systems allow real-time decision making and coordination during complex tasks.
*   Safety mechanisms: Advanced, automated technology like robotics is often followed by immense safety concerns. Emergency stop buttons, fail-safes, and safety barriers are crucial to shut down a robot in case of emergencies, system failures, or mishaps.
    
    Source: [https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/building-blocks-robotics-look-key-components-nextup-community-y6alf/](https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/building-blocks-robotics-look-key-components-nextup-community-y6alf/)
    

All these components work together to create highly perceptive robots that can process information, think, and respond accordingly. Let us move further and explore the importance of robotics.

[Exploring the significance of robotics](#contents.xhtml#sc2_153a)

Robotics is important in today’s world because the times we are heading towards with automation facilitated by technologies like blockchain and AI, require it to give the future its proper shape.

Once we explore the evolution of robotics fully, from simple machines to complex, learning entities, we will see how robots have become more efficient, versatile, and intelligent over time, and how they are changing the world around us. Robotics is important not only because of the technical side of things, though. Along with making a real difference in big industries like manufacturing, logistics, healthcare, and agriculture, robots are also making their way into our homes, helping us with our daily chores, and even keeping us company.

[Importance of robotics](#contents.xhtml#sc3_154a)

Let us talk about the incredible world of robotics and why it is becoming such a big deal in so many sectors.

Robots are not just those cool sci-fi characters we see in movies. They can work non-stop without getting tired, handle tasks that might be dangerous for us, and they are super precise. Here is why they are important:

*   Take manufacturing, for instance. Back in the day, humans did everything, but now, robots are the stars of the show. They are assembling, painting, welding, and even packaging. The best part is that they are super-fast, make fewer mistakes, and never call in sick. Plus, there is this new breed of robots called cobots – they are like the friendly neighbors of the robot world. They work right next to us, lending a hand (or a mechanical arm) when needed.
*   Now, let us talk about healthcare. Robots are making waves here too. The Da Vinci Surgical System pictured in the following [Figure 5.1](#ch05.xhtml#fig5-1) is a super advanced robot that assists surgeons in performing intricate procedures. It is so precise that patients often recover faster and with fewer complications. Robots are also helping patients get back on their feet after accidents or illnesses. They are like high-tech physiotherapists.

![](images/Figure_5.1.png)

Figure 5.1: Three components of the da Vinci system  
Source: [https://www.intuitive.com/en-us/patients/da-vinci-robotic-surgery/about-the-systems](https://www.intuitive.com/en-us/patients/da-vinci-robotic-surgery/about-the-systems)

*   Agriculture's getting a tech makeover too. Robots are out in the fields planting, harvesting, and even keeping an eye on crops. They are like the modern-day scarecrows, but way smarter. With all their sensors and AI, they give farmers real-time updates, helping them make the best decisions for their crops. It is all about growing more while taking care of Mother Earth.
*   Let us talk about logistics. Robots are in warehouses picking, packing, and sorting. Day or night, they are making sure our packages are ready to go. With drones and autonomous delivery bots on the horizon, our packages might just land on our doorsteps even faster.
*   Robots are also making their mark in construction, mining, defense, and even space exploration. Whether it is laying bricks, exploring Mars, or defusing bombs, robots are there, making things safer and more efficient for all of us.

So, in a nutshell, robots are not just machines; they are our partners in progress. They are helping us do more, reach further, and dream bigger.

[Robotics and its history](#contents.xhtml#sc2_155a)

Let us explore the history of robotics and explore when we first saw something closely resembling today’s robots and how robotics evolved over many years.

[Early beginnings of robotics](#contents.xhtml#sc3_156a)

Creating machines that can perform tasks autonomously has been a source of human fascination for centuries. This fascination has its roots in the ancient world, where inventors and engineers crafted automata—mechanical devices that could perform simple tasks or mimic human actions. These early machines were the precursors to the robots we know today, and their creation marked the beginning of humanity's journey toward the development of modern robotics. Let us now talk about the early machines which could carry out various tasks:

*   In ancient Greece, the engineer Heron of Alexandria pioneered the creation of automata, as is referenced in the following [Figure 5.2](#ch05.xhtml#fig5-2). He designed various devices, including a steam-powered contraption that could open temple doors and a mechanical play that could run for ten minutes. While simple by today's standards, these creations were revolutionary for their time. They demonstrated that machines could be designed to perform tasks without human intervention, laying the groundwork for developing autonomous machines.

![](images/Figure_5.2.png)

Figure 5.2: Heron of Alexandria’s diagram of an automaton dispensing wine and milk in a temple  
Source: [https://www.historyofinformation.com/image.php?id=3851](https://www.historyofinformation.com/image.php?id=3851)

*   Similarly, in ancient China, inventors crafted mechanical figurines that could serve tea or play music. Like those of Heron of Alexandria, these automata were powered by simple mechanisms such as weights, springs, and water. They could only perform pre-programmed tasks, and the concept of a machine that could think and learn like a human was still a distant dream. However, these early automata represented an essential step in the evolution of robotics, demonstrating the potential of machines to mimic human actions and perform tasks autonomously.
*   The journey towards modern robotics took a significant leap forward in the 18th and 19th centuries, with the development of programmable machines such as the Jacquard loom and the analytical engine. These machines used punched cards to control their operations, a concept that would later be integral to developing the first computers.

The Jacquard loom, for instance, could weave complex patterns based on the instructions encoded in the punched cards, demonstrating the potential of machines to perform complex tasks with precision and consistency. You can find it pictured in [Figure 5.3](#ch05.xhtml#fig5-3):

![](images/Figure_5.3.png)

Figure 5.3: Jacquard loom in the National Museum of Scotland  
Source: [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacquard\_machine#/media/File:A\_Jacquard\_loom\_showing\_information\_punchcards,\_National\_Museum\_of\_Scotland.jpg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacquard_machine#/media/File:A_Jacquard_loom_showing_information_punchcards,_National_Museum_of_Scotland.jpg)

*   As mentioned back in [Chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml), AI Explained, in Karel Čapek's 1920 play Rossum's Universal Robots (R.U.R.), the term robot was introduced. The play portrayed a future where artificial humans, also known as robots, were employed as low-cost labor. This dystopian vision of a world dominated by robots sparked a rebellion leading to the extinction of the human race, highlighting the potential dangers of unchecked technological advancement.
*   The first actual industrial robot, the Unimate, was installed on a General Motors assembly line in 1961. The Unimate was a programmable machine that could perform repetitive tasks such as welding and moving parts. Its success marked a turning point in the history of robotics. For the first time, a robot was being used in a real-world industrial setting, performing tasks previously done by humans. This marked the beginning of the modern era of robotics, paving the way for the widespread adoption of robots in manufacturing and other industries.

You can find the Unimate picture in the following [Figure 5.4](#ch05.xhtml#fig5-4):

![](images/Figure_5.4.png)

Figure 5.4: Unimate  
Source: [https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/the-unimate-robot/6QHAyki7K0pjZA](https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/the-unimate-robot/6QHAyki7K0pjZA))

The early beginnings of robotics, from ancient automata to the first industrial robots, represent a journey of human ingenuity and innovation. They reflect our enduring fascination with creating machines that can perform tasks autonomously, a fascination that has driven the evolution of robotics from simple mechanical devices to today's sophisticated, intelligent robots.

As we continue to explore the potential of robotics, these early beginnings remind us how far we have come and hint at the exciting possibilities that lie ahead!

[Robotics over the years](#contents.xhtml#sc3_157a)

The shifts in robotics over the years since the ancient automata is a testament to human innovation and the relentless pursuit of technological advancement. From the introduction of the Unimate, the world's first industrial robot, to today's sophisticated, intelligent robots, the journey of robotics has been marked by significant milestones and transformative breakthroughs.

Here is a look at robotics over the years:

*   The 1970s and 1980s marked a pivotal period in the evolution of robotics. During this time, robots transitioned from rigid, inflexible machines to more versatile and intelligent devices. The Stanford Arm and the Programmable Universal Machine for Assembly (PUMA) were prime examples of this shift. These robots, capable of performing precise, complex tasks, demonstrated the potential of robotics in various applications.
    
    The following [Figure 5.5](#ch05.xhtml#fig5-5) shows PUMA:
    
    ![](images/Figure_5.5.png)
    
    Figure 5.5: PUMA  
    Source: [https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programmable\_Universal\_Machine\_for\_Assembly#/media/File:Unimate\_500\_PUMA\_Deutsches\_Museum.jpg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programmable_Universal_Machine_for_Assembly#/media/File:Unimate_500_PUMA_Deutsches_Museum.jpg)
    
    Concurrently, the development of microprocessors and software revolutionized the control mechanisms of robots, enabling more flexible and intelligent operations.
    
*   As we moved into the 1990s and 2000s, robots began to venture out of the factory setting and into the wider world. This period saw the emergence of robots in everyday life and sensitive applications. The Roomba vacuum cleaner, for instance, revolutionized home cleaning. At the same time, the Da Vinci Surgical System brought unprecedented precision and control to surgical procedures. Meanwhile, the Mars rovers Spirit and Opportunity showcased the potential of robots in exploration and scientific research, opening new frontiers in space exploration. These Mars rovers can be seen in the following [Figure 5.6](#ch05.xhtml#fig5-6):

![](images/Figure_5.6.png)

Figure 5.6: Mars rover twins: Spirit and Opportunity  
Source: [https://science.nasa.gov/mission/mars-exploration-rovers-spirit-and-opportunity/](https://science.nasa.gov/mission/mars-exploration-rovers-spirit-and-opportunity/))

*   The most recent years have witnessed yet another revolution in the field of robotics, driven by advancements in artificial intelligence. Today's robots can learn from experience, adapt to new situations, and even understand natural language. AI has opened new possibilities for robots, from self-driving cars and delivery drones to personal assistant robots and robotic companions. These advancements are transforming industries and redefining what robots can do.

Despite these significant advancements, the field of robotics is still in its early stages. There are numerous challenges to overcome, both technical and societal. Improving robot dexterity and autonomy remains a significant challenge on the technical front. Robots must be able to navigate complex, unpredictable environments and perform delicate tasks requiring high precision and control.

On the societal front, ensuring robots' safe and ethical use is a pressing issue. As robots become more integrated into our lives, we must consider the ethical implications and establish guidelines to ensure that the benefits of robotics are realized in a way that puts humans first.

The potential of robotics is vast, and the journey ahead will be exciting and transformative. As we continue to push the boundaries of what robots can do, we expect to see further advancements that will redefine our understanding of robotics and its role in our lives.

[Iterations in robotics design and functionality](#contents.xhtml#sc2_158a)

The new iterations we are seeing in robotics are the result of multifaceted evolution and here are the different kinds.

[Flexibility revolution](#contents.xhtml#sc3_159a)

The clunky, rigid robots of yesteryear have undergone a massive transformation. Today's robots are all about adaptability and versatility. They are designed to be the ultimate problem solvers, ready to tackle diverse challenges. There are two types of robots such as:
