Our objective in this chapter is to understand the importance of green energy and explore the various promising sectors of it. Further, we will explore green blockchains and AI’s partnership with renewable energy. We will also shine a light on the application of the technologies of tomorrow to renewable energy and how many issues that currently exist can be solved. To top it all off, let us also take a look at the challenges that still persist when it comes to blending green energy with emerging technologies, and how those may be tackled in the times to come.

[Introducing green energy](#contents.xhtml#sc2_275a)

Green energy, also known as renewable energy, is any energy derived from resources that are naturally replenished. Such sources of energy include everything from sunlight to wind to rain to tides to waves to geothermal heat, and more.

Unlike conventional sources of energy that would not be replenished (at least not on a human time scale) such as fossil fuels, green energy is far more sustainable and has a lower to zero environmental impact.

The key forms of green energy include:

*   Solar energy is generated by capturing sunlight using solar panels.
*   Wind energy, which is produced by capturing the power of wind through wind turbines.
*   Hydropower, which is generated by capturing the energy from moving water, often in dams or rivers.
*   Geothermal energy, which is extracted from the earth's internal heat.
*   Biomass energy, which is derived from organic materials, such as plant and animal waste.

[Importance of green energy for upcoming technologies](#contents.xhtml#sc2_276a)

The reasons why green energy is important are many and apparent. Let us look at them:

*   Environmental sustainability: 
    *   Green energy sources: They produce much fewer greenhouse gas emissions compared to traditional fossil fuels. This helps mitigate climate change and reduce air pollution, contributing to a more sustainable and environmentally friendly future.
    *   Abundance of resources: Unlike the finite fossil fuel reserves, green energy sources are abundant as they are naturally replenished. So renewable energy ensures a continuous and reliable energy supply, reducing dependence on scarce resources and promoting long-term energy security.
    *   Energy independence for nations: Utilizing green energy sources helps reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels, which enhances energy independence for countries. This can lead to more stable energy prices for consumers, combined with lower vulnerability to geopolitical conflicts related to energy resources.
    *   Technological innovation: Investing in green energy technologies works to foster innovation and drive advancements in energy storage, transmission, and efficiency. The benefits of innovation go beyond just the energy sector and stimulate technological progress in other industries.
*   Job creation: 
    *   Green energy sector: This sector has the potential to create numerous jobs across manufacturing, installation, maintenance, and research. This can contribute to a nation's economic growth and provide brand new opportunities for skilled workers in various industries.
    *   Decentralization of energy: Green energy technologies such as solar panels and wind turbines can be deployed at various scales, which promotes the principles of decentralized energy generation. Overall, green energy reduces the need for extensive centralized power grids and enhances resilience and adaptability.
*   Community empowerment: 
    *   Local level: Green energy projects empower communities to actively participate in and benefit from energy production. This can lead to increased community resilience and economic development.
    *   Reduced environmental impact: Green energy sources have a lower environmental impact compared to conventional sources like fossil fuels, as is only natural. Renewable energy helps conserve ecosystems, preserve biodiversity, and reduce the negative effects of resource extraction associated with traditional energy sources.
    *   Cost competitiveness: Advancements in green energy technologies can lead to cost reductions as time goes by, making renewable energy’s accessibility on par with conventional sources. This cost parity works to enhance the economic viability of green energy solutions.

To put it in brief, green energy can be vital for the technologies of tomorrow because it addresses environmental challenges and promotes sustainability, all key areas technologies like blockchain and AI still struggle with. In fact, renewable energy can be called a cornerstone for building a cleaner, more sustainable future.

[Promising sectors for renewable energy](#contents.xhtml#sc2_277a)

Before a mass adoption of green energy can happen, it is important for the common man to know the various sectors and sources of renewable energy beyond just the names solar and wind energy. Now, let us investigate the various types of renewable energy:

*   Floating solar energy: One form of perhaps the most popular section of renewable energy—solar energy—the first area we discuss is floating solar power. It is exactly what you might imagine: mounting photovoltaic panels upon floating structures sailing on various water bodies. This is particularly useful for areas where large spans of land are either hard to come by or expensive.
    
    ![](images/Figure_9.1.png)
    
    Figure 9.1: China’s 150 megawatt (MW) floating solar PV plant  
    Source: [https://www.climateaction.org/news/china-breaks-ground-on-the-worlds-largest-floating-solar-plant](https://www.climateaction.org/news/china-breaks-ground-on-the-worlds-largest-floating-solar-plant)
    
    For example, China has developed a 150 Megawatt (MW) floating solar PV plant to cut down on the cost of solar energy. The Huainan Floating Solar PV Park 2 is located in Anhui, China, as can be seen in [Figure 9.1](#ch09.xhtml#fig9-1).
    
*   Tidal energy: Tidal energy is another renewable power source generated by making use of the gravitational forces between the earth, moon, and sun to produce electricity. The rise and fall of ocean tides create kinetic energy, which is captured using tidal power systems. These systems typically employ underwater turbines or tidal stream generators to convert the movement of water into electrical energy.
    
    Tidal energy is both predictable and consistent, providing a much more sustainable alternative to conventional fossil fuels. Tidal energy holds significant potential for clean, reliable power generation, contributing to the global shift towards more environmentally friendly energy solutions.
    
    The following figure shows the MeyGen Tidal Stream Project, Scotland:
    
    ![](images/Figure_9.2.jpg)
    
    Figure 9.2: MeyGen tidal stream project, Scotland  
    Source: [https://saerenewables.com/tidal-stream/meygen/](https://saerenewables.com/tidal-stream/meygen/)
    
    For example, The MeyGen project in Scotland is the largest ever planned tidal stream project in the world. The MeyGen project consists of installing tidal turbines in the Pentland Firth, infamous for some of the strongest tides across the world. The project generated about 51 GWh (watt hours) of energy as of March 2023. Notably, the MeyGen offshore lease and site resource can allow a capacity up to 398MW to be installed in the coming times.
    
*   Geothermal energy: Geothermal energy is a renewable power source harnessed from the earth's internal heat. Essentially, this green energy is derived from the natural heat beneath the earth's surface, tapped through geothermal power plants and direct-use applications. By capturing steam or hot water from reservoirs deep underground, geothermal systems generate electricity or provide direct heating for buildings and industrial processes. 
    
    Geothermal energy sources minimize greenhouse gas emissions, offer a continuous and reliable power supply, and play a crucial role in the transition towards cleaner, more sustainable energy alternatives. 
    
    The following figure shows the Blue Lagoon, powered by geothermal energy, Iceland:
    
    ![](images/Figure_9.3.jpg)
    
    Figure 9.3: Blue Lagoon, powered by geothermal energy, Iceland  
    Source: [https://www.thinkgeoenergy.com/icelands-blue-lagoon-expanding-to-meet-growth/](https://www.thinkgeoenergy.com/icelands-blue-lagoon-expanding-to-meet-growth/)
    
    For example, Iceland is the most notable example where geothermal energy is in mass use. In fact, Iceland has a 99.96% renewable energy supply, and about 85% of Iceland’s houses are heated with geothermal energy. Reykjavík, the capital of Iceland, has the biggest district heating system in the world, and the widely renowned Blue Lagoon is completely powered by geothermal energy.
    
*   Hydrogen energy: Yet another promising sector in green energy, hydrogen energy is a clean and versatile form of power derived from hydrogen gas. It serves as a promising alternative to conventional fossil fuels, producing energy with minimal environmental impact.
    
    Hydrogen can be generated through various methods, such as electrolysis using renewable electricity or by reforming natural gas. When used in fuel cells, hydrogen undergoes a chemical reaction with oxygen, producing electricity and water as byproducts. This process, known as electrochemical conversion, releases no harmful emissions.
    
    Hydrogen energy holds potential for diverse applications, including transportation, industrial processes, and electricity generation, contributing to a more sustainable and low-carbon energy future. The following figure shows the hydrogen fuel cell:
    
    ![](images/Figure_9.4.jpg)
    
    Figure 9.4: Hydrogen fuel cell in Toyota Mirai  
    Source: [https://www.toyota-europe.com/electrification/fcev](https://www.toyota-europe.com/electrification/fcev)
    
    For example, one notable example of hydrogen energy in use is in hydrogen fuel cell vehicles. These vehicles, (there are hydrogen-powered cars like the Toyota Mirai or Hyundai Nexo) utilize hydrogen as a fuel to generate electricity through the aforementioned chemical reaction in the fuel cell. As the automotive industry explores sustainable options, hydrogen fuel cell vehicles represent a promising application of hydrogen energy in reducing carbon emissions from transportation.
    
    Aside from these innovative green energy sources, there are some significant renewable energy trends worth looking into. Some of them are as follows:
    
*   Bioenergy with carbon capture and storage (BECCS), which is a technology to combine bioenergy production with the capture and storage of carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions. The overall goal of BECCS is to achieve negative emissions, which means that more CO2 is removed from the atmosphere than is released during bioenergy production. By actively removing CO2, BECCS aims to offset existing emissions and contribute to the global efforts to combat climate change.
    
    ![](images/Figure_9.5.png)
    
    Figure 9.5: Drax Power Station in the UK  
    Source: [https://www.drax.com/about-us/our-sites-and-businesses/drax-power-station/](https://www.drax.com/about-us/our-sites-and-businesses/drax-power-station/)
    
    In the United Kingdom, Drax Power Station is now exploring the usage of BECCS to capture and then store CO2 emissions caused by biomass combustion. Currently, the Drax Power Station comes up with a good 11% of the UK’s renewable energy, as a flexible and secure source as opposed to the more intermittent renewable sources like wind and solar power. The preceding [Figure 9.5](#ch09.xhtml#fig9-5) captures the station:
    
*   Energy storage solutions like lithium-ion batteries and pumped hydro storage are increasingly being used.
    
    ![](images/Figure_9.6.jpg)
    
    Figure 9.6: Tesla powerpack installed in Japan  
    Source: [https://www.tesmanian.com/blogs/tesmanian-blog/tesla-powerpack-in-japan](https://www.tesmanian.com/blogs/tesmanian-blog/tesla-powerpack-in-japan)
    
    For example, the Tesla Powerpack is a lithium-ion battery storage system which stores excess green energy and provides it to the grid at times of high demand. Again, this decreases our dependability on fossil fuels and stabilizes the grid.
    

Now, we have an idea of what green energy is, what promises it holds for the future, and the most exciting sectors of it. It is time for us to move on to how renewable energy can help the technologies of tomorrow turn green.

Let us start with green blockchains.

[Introducing green blockchains](#contents.xhtml#sc2_278a)

As we discussed at the very beginning of this chapter, the Bitcoin blockchain’s carbon footprint is more than an entire country in a year, which has been at the core of blockchains’ sustainability debates for almost as long as Bitcoin has been around. While tech and finance enthusiasts have been quick to point out that the success and widespread necessity of Bitcoin’s use case (which is to facilitate decentralized, peer-to-peer transactions around the world) more than makes up for its energy-intensiveness, a call for green blockchains is still making its way through the crypto community.

The demand has in fact magnified after Ethereum—which used to have the same consensus mechanism as Bitcoin and was therefore similarly energy-intensive- has switched to a different consensus system for a greener approach.

[Bitcoin’s power consumption](#contents.xhtml#sc3_279a)

Bitcoin’s consensus mechanism is proof of work, which utilizes a mining mechanism to achieve consensus and decentralization upon its network. The proof of work (PoW) can be visualized in the following [Figure 9.7](#ch09.xhtml#fig9-7):

![](images/Figure_9.7.png)

Figure 9.7: Proof of work shaping up  
Source: [https://levelup.gitconnected.com/bitcoin-proof-of-work-the-only-article-you-will-ever-have-to-read-4a1fcd76a294)](https://levelup.gitconnected.com/bitcoin-proof-of-work-the-only-article-you-will-ever-have-to-read-4a1fcd76a294\))

The transition to the proof of stake (PoS) consensus mechanism is a viable solution to Bitcoin’s sustainability challenges.

Currently, in Bitcoin’s PoW, miners compete to solve complex mathematical puzzles as we know, validating transactions on the chain and creating new blocks. All these miners run powerful computers to perform these computations, with the first to solve the puzzle every round winning the rights to add a new block to the blockchain. This process of course requires significant computational energy, providing decentralization and security but also raising environmental concerns.

While PoW has a decade behind it to prove its effectiveness in running the biggest blockchain network of today, its energy consumption has spurred interest in alternative consensus mechanisms with lower environmental impact, such as PoS. With PoS, there is no need for participants to run highly powerful computers throughout the day to add new blocks; all they need to do is lock up their native crypto holdings to have a stake in the network and earn rewards for the deposits.

The very first known and functioning use of the PoS algorithm was for the blockchain behind the crypto Peercoin; this was back in 2012. While Peercoin did not exactly earn a lot of recognition, there have been popular blockchains like Cardano and Algorand since that implemented PoS from its introduction itself and have seen good results since then.

The true debate of PoW vs. PoS and sustainable, green blockchains started only after Ethereum transitioned to PoS, however.

Ethereum is a network nearly as large as Bitcoin and being at the heart of the growth of decentralized finance and housing numerous blockchain-based projects. Before Ethereum’s transition to PoS, many cited that a network as big as Bitcoin cannot go through a consensus mechanism transition as smoothly as a smaller network to maintain sustainability, and the blockchain’s decentralized nature might suffer with such a change in any case.

The first reasoning was neutralized by Ethereum’s large scale yet smooth transition to a proof of stake mechanism. With its proof of stake shift, Ethereum managed to cut down on over 99% of its energy consumption as the previous proof of work network, notably.

However, coming to the second, a significant part of the crypto community does not believe PoS to be as decentralized as PoW. This is because in a PoS blockchain, those with higher stakes get to compete as validators and stand to be chosen to add new blocks to the chain. In fact, the higher your stake in a blockchain like Ethereum, the higher chances you have to be selected. This is why validator groups with higher stakes in the network have the potential power to control the blockchain.

For example, Lido is a liquid staking solution which currently commands 31.9% of all staked Ethereum on the blockchain. This is higher than any other platform or individual, and therefore stands as a potential threat to the decentralization of Ethereum. In [Figure 9.4](#ch09.xhtml#fig9-4), you can see the amount of staked ETH across other platforms compared to Lido. So now, while theoretically Bitcoin’s complete transaction to a PoS or any other less energy intensive consensus mechanism is possible despite its large size, there may be compromizations on the decentralization front. Considering Bitcoin has reached mainstream acclaim to an extent where it is legal tender in El Salvador and many other nations currently have pro-Bitcoin politicians at the helm, such a risk to the network might not be acceptable to many.

Let us now talk about the solution to green blockchains and what can be done to ensure decentralization while limiting carbon footprints.

[Achieving green blockchains](#contents.xhtml#sc3_280a)

A green blockchain is essentially one that does not contribute to climate change noticeably. Here are some ways a blockchain like Bitcoin can turn green:

*   Network participants may make a conscious decision at an individual level to only use green energy.
*   A consensus mechanism has to be chosen that does not require a lot of energy for transaction processing and recording in the first place, so the stakeholders in the network do not have to manage carbon footprint on an individual level. However, as we discussed, this might mean a compromization on security.

Using green energy to power blockchain mining can preserve the unique value a PoW-powered network brings to the table. For this, there are the following two requirements:

*   For renewable energy to become cheaper and more accessible.
*   For regulatory requirements to be there for miners to adopt green energy sources.

Alternative energy sources solar, hydro, tidal, geothermal, and biomass are already under development. Meanwhile, energy created through nuclear power is surprisingly at the forefront to initiate green Bitcoin mining. In March 2023, TeraWulf (WULF) initiated operations at the Nautilus Cryptomine facility, which is the first ever nuclear-powered Bitcoin mining facility in the US. The infrastructure is reported to have about 8,000 mining rigs online representing a computing power or hashrate of around 1.0 exahash per second (EH/s).

The benefits to using nuclear energy for mining Bitcoin (or any other proof of work crypto) are numerous, including:

*   Nuclear power brings the highest possible energy capacity of all different power resources.
*   Nuclear power also commands high reliability; certainly, more than wind or solar power, which can be more unpredictable.
*   Nuclear fission is super-efficient and provides about 8,000 times more energy than the conventional fossil fuels method.
*   Nuclear power costs are competitive with the more traditional sources, which makes it a better alternative to other renewable energy courses for the cost-conscious.
*   While many still harbor misconceptions about nuclear power being dangerous, the truth is that the resource releases far less radiation than any other sources. In fact, studies have shown that the very worst nuclear plant accident would still cause less damage than an industrial mishap.

While green Bitcoin/green blockchain’s widespread adoption depends on individual users still, there are certainly several sustainable options ready for exploration. In fact, many Bitcoin community members are already utilizing green energy sources like the geothermal power in East Africa and volcanic activity in El Salvador.

Meanwhile, around 40% of the blockchains active today utilize proof of stake or other consensus mechanisms aside from PoW. This is testament to the viability of green blockchains in the coming times, through a combination of green energy usage and less energy-intensive consensus mechanisms.

[Examples of green blockchains](#contents.xhtml#sc4_281a)

Some of the green blockchains of today include Cardano, Solana, and Polkadot. All three of these blockchains forego PoW and opt for newer consensus systems the likes of PoS, proof of history, and more.

The next frontier for exploration is AI and renewable energy, as green energy significantly enhances the sustainability of AI mechanisms.

[AI and renewable energy](#contents.xhtml#sc2_282a)

While AI firms are responsible for denoting and decreasing their carbon footprint, users should also actively find out the relevant information and choose to put their support behind projects that actively look out for environmental factors.

As we discussed ChatGPT at the beginning of this chapter, if you think about it, it is only natural that a ChatGPT query uses more energy than Google. When you pose a question to ChatGPT, your expected output can only come after the model runs the equivalent of multiple Google searches. However, even as Bitcoin has its reasons to use PoW to protect its decentralized network, the sustainability factor cannot be ignored, and this is a similar instance.

The carbon footprint of AI grows exponentially as applications like ChatGPT, Midjourney, and more become easily accessible; at the end of 2023, ChatGPT records about 180.5 million users. The questions of environmental impact can no longer be ignored.

[Impact of AI models on the environment](#contents.xhtml#sc3_283a)

The following are the reasons AI models are so energy-intensive:

*   When it comes to training AI models, the work is done in data centers across the world where numerous computers are always active to process the data. Quite like mining computers, training activities also require these computers to consume huge amounts of energy.
*   As data from the International Energy Agency suggested, data centers around the world took up about 220-330 TWh of electricity in 2021, and about 240-340 TWh of electricity in 2022. This amounts to about 1-1.3% of the total global electricity demand. Most of these data centers still use energy sourced from fossil fuels, in turn causing massive greenhouse gas emissions and furthering global warming.
*   As was estimated in 2021, Google’s total electricity consumption was about 18.3 TWh or terawatt-hours, with AI having contributed to nearly 10-15% of that total. Hypothetically, Google’s AI could go to an extent soon where it consumes about as much energy as a country like Ireland (which consumes around 30 TWh per year).
*   For large language models in particular, each processing unit in data centers is estimated to take up over 400 watts of power while in operation. A similar amount of power is required for cooling and power management too. Overall, the numbers go somewhere around 10 gigawatt-hour (GWh) for every large language model like ChatGPT. Again, to put into perspective, the number is almost equivalent to the annual electricity consumption of around 1,000 houses in the US.

[Achieving green AI](#contents.xhtml#sc3_284a)

For cooling machinery, many AI data centers also need huge amounts of water instead of air cooling. Water-cooled data centers have been proven to use about ten percent less energy than air-cooled ones, and therefore cause lower carbon footprints.

For instance, in 2021, Google’s global data centers required about 4.3 billion gallons of water. This helped Google reduce the energy-centric carbon footprint left by its data centers by about 300,000 tons of CO2. Similarly, as per Microsoft’s 2022 Environmental Sustainability Report, its global water consumption had gone from 4.7 million cubic meters in 2021 to a sheer 6.4 million cubic meters in 2022. This translated to about 1.7 billion gallons of water, most likely due to its AI research.

As you can gauge, while this lowers the carbon footprint left behind by big AI corporations, it consumes critical water supply from across the world, putting aquatic life in jeopardy too. There are various solutions available.

Well, firstly, using renewable energy to power data centers is part of the solution for a green future. Aside from that, we can choose to use AI services after deliberate consideration. Especially for generative AI, as experts opine, creating casual blog posts and images for personal amusement/gains is not exactly the best use for such energy-intensive tools. As users, we have to focus on using AI to the best of its capabilities in critical areas like medicine and healthcare, and disaster control too, with its predictive and decision-making abilities.

Again, just as with blockchain, taking accountability at an individual level is what we must aim to do.

Green energy significantly benefits from emerging technologies, enabling sustainable growth and innovation for the future. Let us explore that next.

[Applying emerging technologies to renewable energy](#contents.xhtml#sc2_285a)

Now, here is a brand-new aspect we may not have put much thought into before. Blockchain and AI are benefited by the usage of green energy and technologies, but both also have something to give back. Without further ado, let us explore the solutions both of these technologies of the future provide to renewable energy.

[Blockchain in green energy](#contents.xhtml#sc3_286a)

As we have discussed in Achieving green blockchains, there are still challenges when it comes to taking renewable energy to the masses. These challenges have to do with the cost and accessibility of green energy. Before understanding the use case of blockchain in renewable energy, let us explore the challenges that persist.

[Blockchain solutions for green energy challenges](#contents.xhtml#sc4_287a)

The following is the scope for blockchain-based solutions in renewable energy:

*   Handling renewable energy presents challenges due to its inherent instability. Solar and wind power generation fluctuates, for example, posing difficulties for electric power companies to ensure a consistent energy supply. Fluctuations in renewable energy production can affect grid stability, after all.
*   A lack of transparency in the green energy supply chain can raise questions of origin and authenticity. Additionally, verifying the renewable origin incurs costs, as proof of source is required. Needless to say, this is a challenge unique to renewable energy as traditional energy sources do not require such proves.
*   Efficient and transparent mechanisms are a necessity for easy, optimized peer-to-peer energy trading.
*   Incentives for individual energy producers to contribute excess energy to the grid is another need to keep the supply of green energy efficient.
*   The lack of transparency and accountability in carbon credit markets is another issue relevant to the green energy sector.
*   Finally, there may be difficulties in raising funds for renewable energy projects.

Now we see how blockchain solves these issues, although you may already have an idea.

[Blockchain as the solution](#contents.xhtml#sc4_288a)

The following are the solutions provided by blockchain to green energy:

*   Smart contracts on the blockchain can facilitate decentralized grid management, optimizing energy distribution and balancing supply and demand in real-time.
*   The transparent and immutable blockchain ledger ensures traceability of renewable energy sources, preventing any frauds and providing consumers with verifiable information about the origin of energy.
*   The transparency and traceability of blockchain also eliminates the need for costly external verification, making renewable energy more economically viable. By securely documenting the renewable source on the blockchain, the associated proving costs are reduced, fostering wider adoption and potentially lowering prices for renewable energy consumers.
*   Smart contracts enable decentralized energy trading, as we know, allowing consumers with renewable sources to sell excess energy directly to other users on a secure and transparent platform.
*   Tokenization and smart contracts can create a tokenized incentivization system for energy providers, rewarding them for contributing surplus energy and supporting decentralized energy networks.
*   Blockchain can enhance the transparency and traceability of carbon credit transactions, ensuring the legitimacy of emission reduction efforts.
*   Tokenization and crowdfunding through blockchain platforms enable fractional ownership of renewable energy projects, democratizing investment and facilitating decentralized funding for all such projects.

Blockchain serves further use cases in the green energy sector, such as the decentralized and distributed nature of blockchain enhances grid resilience and security by reducing the risk of a single point of failure or cyber-attacks. By addressing all issues of a green energy initiative, blockchain technology overall stands to contribute by much to the advancement and broader adoption of renewable energy, making the transition to sustainable and decentralized energy systems more feasible in the coming times.

Now, it is time to look at the contribution of AI to green energy.

[AI in green energy](#contents.xhtml#sc3_289a)

Off the top of our head, we can surely name at least two aspects of artificial intelligence that can be useful in the energy sector, automation in decision making, and aid to human workers.

Let us explore the different areas where AI can be helpful in the development of renewable energy:

*   AI for automated decision making: With automated decision making, computer systems can process information and present decisions without any sort of human involvement. This way, computers can handle especially complex tasks much faster than humans, that too while saving up on precious resources like time and effort. For instance, automated AI systems can be employed for scheduling preventive maintenance at a solar photovoltaic (PV) plant.
*   AI for aided decision making: Now, automated decision making can bring in different kinds of biases influenced by training data sets, resulting in incorrect decisions that can be potentially hazardous. Therefore, not all sectors of renewable energy can make use of automated decision making. However, AI is still helpful for aided decision making.
    
    This way, the responsibility of the actual decision making/reviewing the final decisions relies on humans who know better and can adapt to different situations with more flexibility. The AI here is only responsible for bringing in insights that can enhance the decision-making process and making suggestions. Therefore, humans and computers can work together and combine their knowledge, experience, and analytic capabilities. Examples of this include areas where nuanced decision making is a must, such as environmental impact analysis at different solar and wind farm sites before a project is finalized.
    
*   AI in identifying risks and opportunities: Image recognition, a part of AI technologies we have discussed before too, can validate data. Therefore, developers and investors can use this technology combined with relevant algorithms to root out all possible risks and opportunities in a planned project. In fact, intelligent algorithms can generate cash-flow models for developers to understand the potential of a project. Since the project modeling process can be automated in its entirety, AI can further identify profitable and potentially successful projects and leave the human part of the team to complete deals for maximum efficiency.
*   AI in output optimization across plants: AI programs can bring in ML weather models, historical data, and real-time information from weather stations, along with satellite imagery, sensors, and cameras to plan and optimize all procedures and output across plants. Well, close to accurate forecasting for weather can allow for the most efficient management for traditional generators and cut down on the expenses of both setting off and shutting down units. This way, plants can adapt expected output to the shifting weather conditions.
*   AI in managing supply and flow: Energy developing companies and grid operators can make use of AI and ML in particular to predict generation of output, schedule maintenance, and even manage energy flows. Consumers, in the meantime, can stay updated about supply so as to manage consumption and storage accordingly to adapt. In fact, smart meters can generate data that AI algorithms use in the future to predict demand and network load as well, managing storage and shifting said load as much as possible to optimize supply.
*   AI in predicting power output: This goes without saying, but AI systems can also use forecasts for things like wind speed to make predictions for short-term outputs in minutes. It is easier with AI integration to manage grid stability and distribution, thus optimizing plant procedures and scheduling maintenance.

As the renewable energy sector is itself a rising one, it is easier for this sector (than already established sectors with set procedures) to adopt emerging technologies like AI and blockchain and build a whole set of procedures around them as an inherent part. Of course, this goes the other way as well. Blockchain and AI are both developing sectors where the use of renewable energy can be well integrated without having to cause many changes to existing protocols.

[Challenges in combining green energy and emerging tech](#contents.xhtml#sc2_290a)

Now, let us discuss some issues we still might have to deal with when it comes to integrating green energy within blockchain and AI, and vice versa:

*   Algorithmic bias in AI systems can result in wrong decision making, which may cause disasters of catastrophic scales, as we have discussed in AI in green energy.
*   Integrating AI and blockchain to optimize renewable energy generation and supply can result in job losses for many. This is a recurring issue with all emerging technologies of today, which can only be resolved by developers/company heads and management tapping into their human nature and making considerations for workers/training them to upskill while optimizing workflows and costs.
*   Data security is one important aspect when it comes to the involvement of AI in green energy, and it can be tackled by having stringent policies in place regarding data anonymity and ensuring transparency for training and decision-making procedures.
*   When it comes to adopting green energy on an individual level, it stands natural that blockchain miners and even AI developers would want to make use of the cheapest energy source available to them, which usually happens to be energy generated by fossil fuels. This issue can only be resolved by making green energy cheaper, more accessible, and adding an incentivization mechanism for selling/consuming green energy.
*   Even when blockchain networks/data centers operated by big AI companies use green energy sources, they can put pressure on generators and take away energy from other critical facilities. In the long term, this might slow down the entire economy's transition to renewable energy efficiency.

These issues remain for the future to tackle as AI and blockchain slowly grow more intertwined with renewable energy.

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml#sc2_291a)

Renewable energy is the future of our world, and it is high time to start consciously fighting climate change and global warming for our future generations to survive in a healthy world. This is precisely why the technologies of tomorrow cannot grow without internalizing green energy as an integral part of themselves. We hope this chapter has told you all about the basics of green energy: what it is, how it is produced, how it can help the emerging technologies like blockchain and artificial intelligence and be helped by them in return.

In the next chapter, we learn all about the privacy and security considerations of the technologies we discuss in this book—from privacy in AI and robotics to the security of blockchain based systems.

[Questions](#contents.xhtml#sc2_292a)

1.  What is renewable energy?
    
    Answer: Renewable or green energy is any energy derived from naturally replenished resources. Examples include solar power and natural gas.
    
2.  How is renewable energy produced?
    
    Answer: Natural resources are harnessed through physical infrastructure to turn them into energy; for example, solar panels are placed to take sunlight and make it into solar energy.
    
3.  Give some examples of major renewable energy plants around the world.
    
    Answer: China's Huainan Floating Solar PV Park 2 is a 150 megawatt (MW) floating solar PV plant, aimed at lowering the cost of solar energy. On the other hand, the MeyGen project in Scotland is the largest tidal stream project in the world.
    
4.  What are the most promising sectors of green energy?
    
    Answer: Floating solar energy, tidal energy, and geothermal energy are some of the most promising sectors of green energy.
    
5.  How is green energy useful to blockchains?
    
    Answer: Green down can make blockchains like the Bitcoin blockchain greener by replacing the fossil fuel and other conventional resources-produced energy used to mine crypto with greener alternatives. This would make blockchains more future-ready.
    
6.  How is renewable energy useful to AI?
    
    Answer: As AI projects grow in number, there are many data centers around the world consuming unprecedented amounts of energy. Renewable energy can help such centers run in a greener fashion, positively impacting the environment.
    
7.  Why does blockchain consume so much energy? What are the different ways this issue can be tackled?
    
    Answer: Blockchain mining involves extremely powerful computers running 24/7 to keep said blockchain networks safe and produce new cryptocurrencies. Blockchains can change their consensus mechanism from proof of work to proof of stake and others to eliminate the mining process, or miners can use green energy to power their computers and protect the environment.
    
8.  How can blockchain optimize green energy and relevant procedures? Which areas in particular does blockchain provide solutions for?
    
    Answer: The transparency and immutability of blockchains ensure no frauds take place and the origin of the energy can be verified, and smart contracts can facilitate decentralized trades, removing any intermediaries. These factors increase faith and lower costs when it comes to green energy.
    
9.  How is AI in renewable energy useful?
    
    Answer: AI can optimize and predict outputs and identify both risks and opportunities, making green energy far more convenient.
    
10.  What are some challenges that lie ahead for the integration of AI and/or blockchain with green energy?
     
     Answer: The higher costs of renewable energy compared to traditional sources and rendering some of the existing employees jobless are two key challenges to tackle.

# [CHAPTER 10Privacy and Security Considerations](#contents.xhtml#ch10a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml#sc2_293a)

It is well established at this point that blockchain, robotics, and AI are the three emerging technologies of the current time that will shape the future of mankind. However, along with their numerous benefits, the latter two of these technologies bring many privacy and security considerations. Our goal in this chapter is to understand what these considerations are, and how these technologies can solve relevant issues—both current and future.

Moreover, we will discuss the potential of blockchain in making data exchange and storage safe in an AI and robotics context. Without further ado, let us get started!

[Structure](#contents.xhtml#sc2_294a)

The chapter covers the following topics:

*   Privacy and security issues in AI
*   Privacy and security issues in robotics
*   Blockchain and data privacy

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml#sc2_295a)

Our objective this time is to first understand which particular security and privacy considerations AI and robotics pose, and to then get into how developers in both sectors can aim to solve these very issues. Further, we will get into the many features of blockchain that ensure secure data transfer and recording in a trustless environment. Our end goal would be to understand how a decentralized ML network can work for better data security and authenticity, with a few examples.

[Privacy and security issues in AI](#contents.xhtml#sc2_296a)

Let us start with AI, the technology that comes under fire the most for causing privacy concerns regarding the data fed to AI systems to enable them to perform various actions. What are the specific issues with AI, and how does it affect privacy and security for humans?

[AI and privacy issues](#contents.xhtml#sc3_297a)

Here is a quick look at some of the aforementioned issues:

*   Violation of copyright and IP laws: AI models are fed data live on the very depths of the world wide web. In the process, copyrighted work and intellectual property from different creators is used. Due to a lack of transparency in the training process, it is rarely possible for such creators to object before the AI model is trained illegally. As AI models grow more refined with time, even their creators lose control over the data being consumed by them, and the legality of such actions.
*   Bias and lack of fairness: AI algorithms might be inheriting biases from the training data fed to them, which could lead to potentially discriminatory outcomes. This can result in fairness issues, such as in applications like hiring or lending.
*   Use of biometric information without consent: As the age of smartphones has come about, the use of biometric data to secure devices has become increasingly common for most of us. Now, along with the convenience of this feature, a pressing issue has also emerged: public surveillance devices may use AI to scan biometric data to detect people efficiently, including security cameras, face or fingerprint scanners, and microphones that identify voices, and they can also store this data. Now, there is little to no regulation yet on how AI companies are allowed to use this collected biometric data. Usually, this data is collected by AI without our consent, and then stored—potentially leading to security breaches unbeknownst to us.
*   Use of any and all user data: Aside from violating user privacy to train AI models, such systems can also be programmed to record data users input as queries and use it for future training. Therefore, there is a chance any data users share with AI models can be used to generate future outputs by the systems without consent. This is particularly risky when users input sensitive data for their query.
*   Use of public records: Public records of various companies and individuals—digitized or otherwise—are frequently used to train AI models. Therefore, current and previous information regarding any entity can be collected by AI without specific consent and be used as a part of the output an AI system generates.
*   Secure data storage: Bigger datasets used in AI need secure storage and transmission. Failure to achieve this would result in data leaks.
*   Lack of security protocols in AI models: Many AI models utilized today do not have built-in security protocols, which means bad actors get the opportunity to unfairly access other users’ personal information. Further, AI models can be susceptible to attacks, such as ones that manipulate inputs to deceive AI systems. Ensuring the security of AI models is essential.
*   IoT and AI integration and relevant security issues: IoT devices will soon be a part of our daily lives, and since AI is integrated into IoT devices, security vulnerabilities in these devices can lead to data breaches and unauthorized access to connected systems.
*   Deepfakes: AI can generate convincing deepfake videos and audio, as witnessed recently with several public figures. This can make it challenging to discern reality from manipulated content. Thus, AI can be used to spread misinformation.
*   Lack of right regulations: Most countries do not have a defined set of rules for AI companies training and building AI models, which means there is no right way to hold involved parties accountable for privacy and security breaches for users all over the internet. So far, AI companies are allowed to decide on their data recording and storage policies by themselves with limited transparency, and following cybersecurity measures depends on their choices too.

Further, the usage of AI in surveillance and monitoring brings in ethical questions regarding the right to privacy. In smart cities and any public spaces, this is yet another qualm to solve before mainstream AI usage can come into being.

[Solving privacy and security concerns related to AI](#contents.xhtml#sc3_298a)

If you think about it, most of the aforementioned issues can be solved if the AI companies in question choose to follow ethical standards and opt for transparency in the training process for their respective models, as well as declare which data they are choosing to enrich the models. However, without the right regulations in place, there is no guarantee that the best practices would be followed.

However, here are some key steps an AI company can follow in the meantime to address AI security issues:

*   Data encryption for any and all information used by AI systems, so it is protected from breaches both while in storage and transit.
*   Data privacy can further be protected with techniques like federated learning and multi-party computation at the time of AI model training.
*   Test the AI models against common cyberattacks with relevant inputs to root out vulnerabilities and remove them.
*   Plan out and stick to ethical AI guidelines with factors like fairness, accountability, and transparency in mind before development.
*   To magnify the transparency factor, make sure the AI models built are easily explainable, so users can understand which data needs to be collected and how and why certain decisions are made. This would help them provide relevant consent and withhold personal information.
*   Publish transparency reports on a regular basis so users have an idea of how their data is being used.
*   Minimize the data you collect and remove or at least limit any retention of personal data.
*   As a rule of thumb, never use any sensitive information for businesses/individuals as training data for an AI model.
*   Users should be made aware of which data they can input and share and what kind of data to never put when engaging with an AI model. 
*   Ensure that any APIs used to access AI services are secure and have maximum protection from hacks.
*   Plan for governance frameworks and oversight mechanisms that would monitor AI models and make sure all processes involved are meeting privacy and security requirements.
*   This goes without saying, but strictly limit control access to sensitive data and the AI system in general.
*   Regular security audits and vulnerability checks must be done to identify or predict vulnerabilities in AI.
*   Further, AI systems should be kept updated with required, new security protocols for maximum protection.
*   You should always have a disaster response plan in case of any security issues, and have clear recovery procedures thought out.
*   Further, collaborate with contemporaries as well as regulatory organizations to determine collective best practices to address AI security challenges.

Aside from these generic approaches, it is important to lay out best AI practices that suit the kind of culture you want to build in your organization and take into account your users’ privacy expectations. Further, it is integral to stay up to date on new kinds of cyber-attacks, regulations, and global practices to adapt to the changing technological landscape. 

[Real world example: Generative AI and solving security issues](#contents.xhtml#sc3_299a)

Before we move on to learning about robotics and relevant security concerns, let us look at the example of the up-and-coming generative AI, what particular issues it faces, and how security issues here are being addressed.

[Generative AI](#contents.xhtml#sc4_300a)

Generative AI is one sub sector of artificial intelligence that makes use of existing datasets to identify patterns and examples to generate new information, graphical representations, and more. As ChatGPT does, generative AI can generate text in response to queries, and further help with creating images, designs, audio and video files, and more. In [Figure 10.1](#ch10.xhtml#fig10-1), you can form an idea of how the mechanisms of generative AI work:

![](images/Figure_10.1.png)

Figure 10.1: Working of generative AI

[Privacy and security concerns in generative AI](#contents.xhtml#sc4_301a)

A generative AI model like ChatGPT can record data input and generated during interactions with users. As you may have realized, generative AI models could thus take in personal data and in the form of outputs later, potentially publish any sensitive information collected. Therefore, the usage of sensitive data (from financial information to medical records to any other private data) in training generative AI can cause privacy violations and put many entities at risk.

[Solving privacy issues related to generative AI](#contents.xhtml#sc4_302a)

As OpenAI, the parent company to ChatGPT does, user privacy and data security should be the two most important factors to keep in mind when building a generative AI model. OpenAI anonymizes and then aggregates collected data to lower the chances of recognizing individuals based on their previous input. Further, they retain collected data for a very limited period and follow security measures to prevent breaches, along with complying with relevant data privacy regulations.

Here are the best practices for solving privacy issues related to generative AI in particular:

*   Train both employees and users to use the tool responsibly. The importance of maintaining privacy in particular must be relayed. While employees should know the risks of manipulating personal user data, users should know which data to use as input and which not to.
*   Anonymizing data and minimizing data collection as OpenAI does is important, along with implementing data security protocols.
*   Compliance with data privacy regulations should be ongoing, as well as frequent monitoring.

Again, protecting data privacy and ensuring security relies largely on the AI company building a system for a certain purpose, as OpenAI’s example clearly demonstrates.

[Privacy and security issues in robotics](#contents.xhtml#sc2_303a)

On to robotics, this technology brings similar privacy concerns as artificial intelligence, especially with how intertwined the two technologies are growing.

[Robotics and privacy and security issues](#contents.xhtml#sc3_304a)

There are many security concerns in relation to robotics that could affect both the machines deployed and the humans around. Some of these challenges include the following, as outlined by select researchers from the American University of Beirut and the University Bourgogne Franche-Comté in their report titled Robotics cyber security: vulnerabilities, attacks, countermeasures, and recommendations.

*   Lack of secure networking: A lack of regulated security protocols could tamper with the communication between humans and robots and potentially make room for different cyber and physical threats.
*   Authentication shortcomings: Failure to follow the right authentication measures and using weak passwords in the place of TLS/SSL or SSH machine identities can cause a robotics system to be manipulated with unauthorized control.
*   Privacy and confidentiality concerns: A lack of confidentiality can emerge out of weak encryption algorithms, which might cause mishandling of sensitive data and design plans. Further, a lack of appropriate security measures can leave sensitive, personal data and intellectual property vulnerable to unauthorized breaches.
*   A lack of integrity: This may come out of the implementation of weak message authentication protocols that can be hacked without much effort, again leading to manhandling of sensitive data, either in storage or in transit.
*   Machine identity verification: Not having robust machine identity verification systems could cause abuse of privilege and unauthorized access to robotic systems.
*   Restrictions on physical access: There should be restrictions on physical access in place to prevent unauthorized access to controls in the labs where robots are built, as well as their working environments.
*   Safety designs: These should be in place to prevent any hazards to humans and the environment, and a lack of them could cause unprecedented harm.
*   Secure by-design features: A lack of these features can cause attackers to break into a robot’s structure and design to exploit any vulnerabilities and initiate consequent attacks. This threat could lead to data poisoning and other unethical alterations to the robotic system.

[Solving privacy and security concerns related to robotics](#contents.xhtml#sc3_305a)

Now, there are many threats and challenges to solve in robotics to prevent unauthorized access to sensitive information and physical harm to humans around the robotic systems, as well as the environment. Let us see how that can be achieved:

*   Secure communication protocols should be built for connectivity between robots, controllers, and any other connected devices. This should put a stop to data interception.
*   The data transmitted between robots and authorized control systems should be encrypted. The same measure can be adopted for stored data to prevent unauthorized access.
*   Strict access controls should be implemented to restrict physical or remote access to robotic systems. Only authorized personnel should be given access to robot control interfaces.
*   Multi-factor authentication could be used to make sure only those with exclusive access can interact with robots and access stored sensitive information.
*   Physical security measures could be adopted to safeguard robots from any manhandling or theft.
*   Secure authentication mechanisms can be built into robots as well, to make sure that they can only adhere to commands from authenticated sources.
*   Like AI systems, only the minimum, necessary data should be collected and stored for a robot’s designated area of work.
*   Robotic software should be regularly updated to keep up with new security measures and updated to address existing vulnerabilities.
*   Privacy considerations can be incorporated during the design and development of robotic systems. For instance, data anonymization and minimization of data collection (especially for any identifiable information for individuals) can be implemented.
*   Logs can be kept for finding out who accesses a robot's data and control systems to immediately catch the unauthorized/unusual activities.
*   Regular security audits and assessments for threats and vulnerabilities can be done on robots to root out any room for exploitation.
*   Development of individual ethical frameworks for robotics systems that focus on the factors of transparency, fairness, and accountability is recommended.

Similar to the measures to address the ethical considerations of AI, the privacy and security challenges of robotics require a proactive approach on the end of developers. Meanwhile, it is important to stay informed regarding evolving regulations as well as threats, and adopt renewed practices to ensure transparency and authenticity.

[Blockchain and data privacy](#contents.xhtml#sc2_306a)

Compared to the two aforementioned technologies, blockchain has not raised many ethical considerations till date. However, there are several elements of blockchain that can enhance data privacy and security when combined with both AI and robotics.

[Decentralization to ensure data security and authenticity](#contents.xhtml#sc3_307a)

Here are some features of blockchain that bring decentralization to support data privacy:

*   Blocks: Blockchain data is stored on virtual blocks, and all transactions are time stamped and signed by the creators. Further, each block contains a unique identifier in its header, and blocks stay on the chain in a chronological order. The immutability of blocks further ensures data authenticity by discouraging tampering.
*   Transparency: The transparency of blockchains help track the origin of any data, as well as the users who have interacted with it. This certainly solves the traceability and transparency requirements for both AI and robotic systems.
*   Hashing: All blocks on the blockchain contain a cryptographic hash of the previous block, which helps maintain the aforementioned chronological order and brings an equipped system to deter bad actors causing data manipulations.
*   Decentralization: The decentralization of blockchains means that there is no single point of failure. The worldwide network of nodes ensures that attackers cannot bring down the entire network altogether and target the data stored.
*   Encryption of data: The encryption of data on blockchain ensures that the data stored on the network is inaccessible to any unauthorized person.
*   Consensus mechanisms: Any changes to a blockchain need a consensus from the majority of the nodes to be carried forward. This means that any unauthorized alterations cannot be done without notifying the entire network of the change, preventing most cyber-attacks.
*   Public and private keys: Every node on the blockchain is given one public key and one private key, the former to receive transactions and the latter to authorize them. This helps ensure user security while still holding transfers back to their origin.

With these features, blockchain can ensure secure data transfers in a trustless, decentralized environment, allowing most of the privacy and security considerations of AI and robotics to be dismissed. With blockchains, you can be sure of data authenticity, limited authorization, and maximized safeguard against security breaches by alerting the entire network of nodes. Data shared through blockchains can be kept safe and only accessed by authorized personnel, and further, a factor of anonymity can be added to the shared data with the respective node using their private key to sign their transactions instead of revealing their name.

In the section ahead, we look at how the combination of blockchain, AI, and robotics can define data security and privacy in the times ahead, along with solving for their respective considerations.

[Blockchain, AI, and robotics in data security and privacy](#contents.xhtml#sc3_308a)

If we start with the issue of deepfakes, blockchain has the power to put a stop to AI-generated fake images or other media files with its immutability and traceability when it comes to data sharing and recording. As the combined market for blockchain and AI-robotics technologies expands—the global blockchain AI market is expected to be valued at $3,519.23 million by 2032- the various features of blockchain continue to bring new data security and privacy measures.

Let us take a look at how some innovative blockchain-based projects are enabling data security and authenticity in this space:

*   Decentralized autonomous organizations (DAOs): VIDT DAO: A blockchain based open-source platform called VIDT DAO works to bring the decentralized timestamping technology of blockchains to keep safe virtual data like certificates, firmware, and AI results, as well as verify their authenticity. The platform’s native token is called the VIDT token, which is used to provide data validation and governance rights across the organization.
    
    Here is how the process works: a VIDT holder timestamps a file they want to keep safe, and then initiates a transaction to record the file and all relevant details into multiple blockchains.
    
    The platform has also expanded its services into FileValidator, a tool which allows you to timestamp your files in the most user-friendly fashion. 
    
    Now, how does VIDT DAO help AI and robotics applications? FileValidator can timestamp AI prompts and the consequent results with its technology, thus keeping transparent and immutable records of the performance of an AI model.
    
*   Trustless data exchange with Fetch.ai: You may remember this particular platform from our previous chapters. Fetch.ai makes use of technologies like blockchain, machine learning, and multi-agent systems to magnify data authenticity, privacy, and security.
    
    The AI company brings a secure infrastructure for autonomous services. Through Fetch.ai, users can build and connect smart agents with different platforms to automate regular tasks or otherwise. Fetch.ai allows users to create open services with the help of AI and automation in multiple sectors, which the ML network ensures blockchain powered data security, privacy, and authenticity.
    
*   AI in market intelligence: LunarCrush: In a slightly different example, LunarCrush is a crypto-asset intelligence platform that makes use of AI to analyze Big Data live on different social media platforms to provide market insights, analytics, and predictions in real time for crypto. This helps even the novice in the crypto space to make informed trading decisions, and the experienced avoid pitfalls that come out of being influenced by fake news and making hasty decisions.

All three of the aforementioned projects help ensure data authenticity and security in the blockchain, AI, and robotics sectors while also keeping fake information at bay. What this demonstrates is that while the emerging technologies have security and ethical considerations of their own to tackle, they can still be used to power the coming decentralized age with user security, transparency, authenticity, and accountability, resulting in a better future for us all.

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml#sc2_309a)

In conclusion, both AI and robotics bring challenges in regard to data security, privacy, and authenticity, as well as authorization of access and ethical issues. However, once combined with blockchain, not only can these issues be solved, but completely new frontiers open up when it comes to secure data transmission and storage in a decentralized, trustless environment. As all three examples in the last section of our chapter demonstrate, decentralized ML networks can indeed bring better data authenticity and security, as compared to the systems we have today.

In the next chapter, we will discuss the industry applications of blockchain, AI, and robotics, along with some case studies. Further, we will delve into the ethical and legal implications of the technologies combined, and the challenges that still stand in the way from legal and other viewpoints. Do stay tuned!

[Questions](#contents.xhtml#sc2_310a)

1.  What are some security considerations of AI?
    
    Answer: Use of biometrics data without consent, violation of copyright and IP laws, and lack of security protocols are some of the primary considerations.
    
2.  What are some measures an AI company can take to eliminate privacy and security challenges in AI?
    
    Answer: Testing AI models against common cyber-attacks, publishing transparency reports, and foregoing the use of sensitive information can help.
    
3.  What is generative AI, and how can the specific security challenges stemming from it be solved?
    
    Answer: Generative AI is a sub-sector of AI which uses existing datasets to figure out patterns and can generate new information, graphical representations, and more as per the commands given.
    
    Anonymizing data, minimizing data collection, and compliance with data privacy regulations can help generative AI be more secure.
    
4.  What kind of security and privacy considerations does robotics bring?
    
    Answer: A lack of confidentiality from weak encryption algorithms and deficiencies in regulated security protocols are two pressing concerns.
    
5.  How can we aim to solve privacy and security issues relevant to robotics?
    
    Answer: Secure communication protocols and encryption for the data transmitted between robots and authorized control systems can help solve these issues.
    
6.  Which are the features of blockchain that can secure our interactions with AI and robotic systems?
    
    Answer: Blockchains can ensure transparency of data as well track its origin and all transactions relevant to it. Further, the cryptographic principles followed on blockchain ensure that the data stored on it are inaccessible to anyone not authorized.
    
7.  Give an example of the combination of blockchain, AI, and robotics ensuring secure data exchange and storage in a trustless environment.
    
    Answer: Fetch.ai utilizes blockchain, machine learning, and multi-agent systems and works to ensure data authenticity, as well as remove privacy and security concerns.
    

# [CHAPTER 11Industry Insights and Implications](#contents.xhtml#ch11a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml#sc2_311a)

In the last chapter, we have talked about privacy and security considerations of AI and robotics and how blockchain steps in to solve most of them. However, as we near the end of our journey in this book, it is time to discuss further ethical and legal implications faced by all emerging technologies of today and the roadblocks keeping them from reaching the common man just yet. Further, we are going to discuss some industry applications for all technologies we have discussed in this book with case studies to truly put into perspective how they are being put to use so far and what challenges remain to be solved.

From regulatory opacity to ethical dilemmas yet to be addressed, there are undoubtedly many issues for developers involved in the emerging technologies of today to tackle. However, while these challenges find their resolutions, AI, blockchain, robotics, and automation are already employed across industries throughout the world. Without further ado, let us make our way through these examples.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml#sc2_312a)

The chapter covers the following topics:

*   Industrial applications of AI, blockchains, robotics, and automation
*   Ethical and legal issues surrounding the technologies of tomorrow
*   Challenges and roadblocks for the future

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml#sc2_313a)

Our goal this time is to discuss the industry applications of the emerging technologies being discussed in this book, paired with some case studies. Moving on from there, we will be exploring the ethical and legal issues AI, robotics, automation, and blockchain face as roadblocks on their way to mainstream success. While we shine a light on these issues and explore how they can be solved, we will also be taking a look at any upcoming challenges we as a society may need to prepare ahead for.

[Applications of AI, blockchain, robotics, and automation](#contents.xhtml#sc2_314a)

All four of these technologies are being put to a variety of industry use cases for a while now. However, there are some use cases in particular that we will highlight in this chapter with some case studies. Let us start with AI first.

[Industry applications of AI and case studies](#contents.xhtml#sc3_315a)

Threaded closely with Internet of Things (IoT), AI-enabled tools and technologies are advantageous for numerous industrial usages. Let us look at some of them:

*   AI in predictive maintenance: One of the most common use cases of AI for industrial purposes, predictive maintenance is basically making use of tech like machine learning to determine whether different assets in a certain place like a factory, or maybe just different parts of a single asset, are performing well. The assets are thus enabled to predict when any maintenance is required.
    
    In this use case, predictive maintenance makes use of multiple sensor readings, external data sources, and more to perform analytics on numerous logged events. ML is further used to determine the remaining life of the asset being supervised.
    
    Real-world example: Deutsche Bahn, a German railway operator, has used data gathered from railway switches to predict any failures. This helped them curb instances of unexpected delays. Moreover, Nissan operates an AI predictive maintenance platform to determine remaining useful life for thousands of assets. The company has claimed an unplanned downtime reduction of 50%, as well as a payback period of less than three months with the implementation of AI for predictive maintenance.
    
*   AI in manufacturing process optimization: Autonomous machines can be put to use for manufacturing process optimization across industries. Intelligent robots here can take over monotonous or hazardous human tasks, saving both time and costs. The reinforcement learning technique is used in such cases to train robots/any autonomous machines, so they perform the tasks in question over and over for accuracy once they are implemented.
    
    Real-world example: ABB has opened a state-of-the-art robotics mega factory in Shanghai where robots are put to use to manufacture more robots. The autonomy of these robots is powered by AI and the concept of digital twin; this twin is essentially a computer program that makes use of real-world data to create simulations and predict how a certain product/process will perform.
    
*   AI in quality inspection: There are several ways to implement AI in quality inspection, but automated optical inspection is perhaps the most utilized as an industrial AI use case. In this, a camera is enabled to autonomously scan a device and test it for any missing components or quality defects that may cause a failure. Computer vision is the particular component of AI technologies put to use here, of course.
    
    Once the images are received, ML is employed to classify the images based on possible failures. Again, both time and costs are saved with this implementation, especially for large manufacturing facilities.
    
    Real world example: A German automotive company has introduced a deep learning powered system for quality control in manufacturing processes. The system is integrated with the final assembly process, and thus renders a special testing area unnecessary.
    
*   AI in cybersecurity: AI finds one of its more popular use cases in ensuring cybersecurity and privacy through cyber threat detection and subsequent processes. In this, AI systems observe a network infrastructure and detect anomalies/malicious activities through their analysis of historical data. The scope of work for AI further includes network traffic analysis, malware sandboxes, endpoint detection and responses, and more.
*   AI in supply chain optimization: There are multiple ways AI is implemented to optimize a supply chain’s activities, namely:
    
    *   Improving inventory management with AI tools to reduce delays.
    *   Predictive analysis to curb inventory planning time and minimize costs.
    *   Predictive analysis to also optimize repairs and root out ideal points for reorders.
    
    For these tasks, the techniques used usually involve time-series analysis, probabilistic modeling, and simulations.
    
    Real-world example: Continental has created software systems to predict the ideal points of time for tire changes for its fleet. The AI model can perform tasks from predicting overall mileage and putting it up against the baseline to initiating actions. With this system, Continental has been able to lower its stock of tires, in turn improving road safety.
    
*   AI in physical security of equipment and workers: Speaking of industrial use cases, AI is able to detect physical threats to both equipment and workers at manufacturing sites through real-time surveillance. AI can leverage computer vision to analyze worker movements and detect anomalies. For instance, it can identify if a worker is not wearing the required safety gear or if there are unauthorized individuals in restricted areas.
    
    Further, predictive analysis can be applied to foresee potential safety hazards, enabling proactive measures to prevent accidents or injuries. Wearable devices equipped with AI techniques can also monitor workers' vital signs in real time, providing quick alerts in case of health emergencies.
    
*   AI-enabled assistants improving workflow: In manufacturing, voice assistants can be integrated for workers to access any information without having to code in the exact commands or going through long reports. AI-powered voice assistants can be incorporated into real-time monitoring systems. Moreover, these assistants can evolve to understand context and provide personalized assistance to workers. This way they can offer step-by-step guidance in case of complex assembly processes or troubleshooting procedures.
    
    Integration with IoT devices can allow voice assistants to control and monitor machinery by themselves, bringing in a more streamlined workflow.
    
*   AI in research and development: AI algorithms can analyze vast datasets to figure out patterns and trends, aiding in the rapid development of new products and components. AI-powered research and development can allow for automated designing of various components. For instance, generative AI would be able to develop multiple different designs for a certain application within a limited period of time, following preset rules regarding factors like cost, materials, and performance. After that, all that remains is to pick the most suitable one. The concept of digital twins and simulations are again used in such applications with AI tools.
*   AI in data management: Data is usually stored across multiple systems in a manufacturing enterprise, making it hard to access on a short notice. However, AI-driven data management systems can automate the extraction, cleaning, and integration of data from diverse sources, providing a unified and accessible data repository.
*   AI in resource exploration: AI can be employed in mining and quarrying and automate resource exploration. areas, streamlining the exploration process. Drones equipped with AI-powered image recognition can survey large areas efficiently, providing valuable insights for selecting optimal locations for mining activities. AI can further be applied to automate the analysis of drilling data, helping in the identification of valuable minerals and streamlining the decision-making process in resource extraction.

These AI applications all contribute to the transformation and increased efficiency of industrial processes, making them responsive to dynamic challenges. As technology continues to advance, the integration of AI in industrial operations is very likely to lead to further innovative use cases, not to mention enhancement of the existing applications.

Now, time to study some industry applications of blockchain.

[Industry applications of blockchain and case studies](#contents.xhtml#sc3_316a)

Blockchain is a relatively newer technology compared to AI, robotics, and automation. However, it has already found its use across varied industries. Let us take a look at some blockchain industrial applications and case studies:

*   Blockchain for supply chain optimization: Finding reliable suppliers can prove to be difficult for businesses at times, resulting in delays and interruptions in supply chains. Further concerns include adherence to quality standards, compliance with the right regulations, and also compliance with relevant ESG measures. Keeping all this in mind, finding the right supplier can be both time consuming and expensive, since acquiring and verifying data from providers without disclosing important information publicly is a significant challenge. Blockchain can be employed to make data sharing simpler with enhanced privacy, however, benefitting all parties involved.
    
    Real-world example: Trust Your Supplier, a supplier information network, streamlines supplier verification for businesses. They have partnered with IBM to create a blockchain application that lets businesses share data with permissioned partners both securely and quickly. The platform allows businesses to have their data authenticated from third-party verifiers in terms of business-related information, financial data, or ESG data. Upon verification, a company is granted a blockchain-based corporate digital passport. 
    
    This use case has reportedly lowered supplier onboarding duration by over 70%, and lessened the cost for data verification for businesses by around 50%.
    
*   Blockchain for compliance in automotive sector: The automotive sector in particular has a large number of stringent regulations to adhere to, including safety rules, geometric features, the quality of material used, environmental considerations, and more. These regulations are subject to quick changes, which need to be communicated to several tiers of suppliers to ensure all equipment is built according to the most updated guidelines. Ensuring compliance can thus prove difficult, and blockchain can be of help again for automotive manufacturers on this front.
    
    Real-world example: Renault also collaborated with IBM to create an ecosystem that can ensure compliance throughout the supply chain in a transparent manner—the first of its kind in the automotive industry. The blockchain application keeps track of all components involved in the manufacturing of automobiles for regulatory compliance. As has been reported, this has cut down on expenses of non-compliance by half, and the expenses of managing non-quality or non-compliance have been reduced by 10%. Since the initiative has proven successful, Renault now has plans to invest further in blockchain for accurate estimations of product carbon footprints and greater visibility in recycling operations.
    
*   Blockchain for international trades: In import and export, international trading can harbor some risks. While an advanced payment from an importer may result in the exporter running away with the money without sending the promised goods, an exporter may also be duped if they send materials first and the importer backs out of making payments. Blockchain, as you may be able to guess, provides a good extent of transparency here, helping importers and exporters get into trustless trades bound by smart contracts.
    
    Real-world example: The Marco Polo Network is making use of blockchains to create a platform where importers and exporters can transparently share data related to orders and delivery through an integration with supply chain ERP systems. The platform creates an immutable contract for counterparties that guarantees payment/supply of goods under conditions agreed upon by both parties, solving all trust issues.
